Conditioning the root to be open changes the survival probability by a factor $p$ and does not change the critical probability. This model gives an independent comparison process in the <independent oriented-percolation comparison for the contact process>.

= Oriented bond percolation
{parent=Directed percolation}
{title2=$p_c^{\mathrm{bond}}$}

Independently open the bonds of a <directed graph> with a common <probability> $p$, and ask whether an infinite open directed path starts at a specified root. On the oriented <square lattice>, edges run from $(x,n)$ to $(x\pm1,n+1)$ with $x+n$ even. The critical probability is the infimum of parameters with positive survival probability. The <incoming-edge coupling of site and bond percolation> compares this threshold with that of <oriented site percolation>.

= Parallel-bond ray with different site and bond thresholds
{parent=Directed percolation}

Take a <directed graph> with vertices $v_0,v_1,\ldots$ and $n+1$ parallel bonds from $v_n$ to $v_{n+1}$. This is a <locally finite graph>, although the incoming degrees are unbounded. For <bond percolation>, $\theta_b(p)=\prod_{n\ge0}(1-(1-p)^{n+1})>0$ whenever $p>0$: the failure <probabilities> are summable, so the <infinite product> is positive. For <site percolation>, survival requires every vertex of the ray to be open, which has <probability> zero for $p<1$. Thus the <survival and susceptibility thresholds for directed percolation> satisfy $p_H^b=0$ and $p_H^s=1$.

= Incoming-edge coupling of site and bond percolation
{parent=Directed percolation}

Suppose every vertex of a <directed graph> has at most $\Delta$ incoming bonds. In <bond percolation> with parameter $q$, open a site whenever at least one incoming bond is open. Incoming bond sets have different heads and hence are disjoint, so these site indicators are <independent random variables>. Supplement their <probabilities> to $r=1-(1-q)^\Delta$. Every vertex reached by a nontrivial open <directed path> is now open. Conditioning the root to be open gives $\theta_s(r)\ge r\theta_b(q)$, since a simple <directed path> from the root never uses a bond entering the root. Thus $p_H^s\le1-(1-p_H^b)^\Delta$.

= Survival and susceptibility thresholds for directed percolation
{parent=Directed percolation}
{title2=$p_H,p_T$}

For the out-cluster $C_x$ in <directed percolation>, define $p_H=\inf\{p:\mathbb P_p(|C_x|=\infty)>0\}$ and $p_T=\inf\{p:\mathbb E_p|C_x|=\infty\}$, with value $1$ when the relevant set is empty below $1$. The first is a survival threshold; the second is a <percolation susceptibility> threshold. Both depend on the root in a general <directed graph>. Exploring a <site percolation> cluster and using each fresh vertex coin for its first tested incoming bond gives a <coupling of probability distributions> with <bond percolation> such that $\theta_s(p)\le p\theta_b(p)$ and $\chi_s(p)\le p\chi_b(p)$. Consequently $p_H^b\le p_H^s$ and $p_T^b\le p_T^s$.

= Conformal invariance of planar percolation
{parent=Percolation theory}

For critical <site percolation> on the <triangular lattice>, scaling-limit crossing probabilities transform naturally under conformal maps between planar domains. This rigorously established property supports descriptions of interfaces by <SLE> and calculations of planar <percolation critical exponents>. Transferring it to a different lattice or to <bond percolation> requires a separate universality argument.

= Lace expansion
{parent=Percolation theory}

An iterative expansion of long-range connectivity functions that separates an approximately random-walk contribution from corrections caused by loops and intersections. In suitable high-dimensional <percolation> models it controls these corrections and proves <mean-field percolation exponents>. It also applies to other models, including <self-avoiding walks>; its use always requires model-specific estimates.

\Include[bond-percolation]{parent=Percolation theory}

= Percolation probability
{title2=$\theta(p)$}
{parent=Percolation theory}

For independent <bond percolation> on an infinite <graph> with a specified root $o$, let $C_o$ be its <percolation cluster>. The percolation probability is
$$
\theta(p)=\mathbb P_p(|C_o|=\infty).
$$
It concerns the root, rather than the existence of an <infinite percolation cluster> somewhere else. Even above the <percolation critical probability> of the <square lattice>, an isolated root has <probability> $(1-p)^4$, because its four incident <edges> can all be closed. Thus $\theta(p)\leq1-(1-p)^4<1$ when $p<1$. Positive percolation probability does not mean every <vertex> is connected to infinity.

On the <square lattice>, the <events> that $o$ is joined by an <open path> to the boundary of $[-n,n]^2$ decrease as $n$ increases. Their <probabilities> converge to $\theta(p)$ by <continuity from above of a measure>. This relates an infinite question to finite observations, without equating any one finite boundary connection with survival forever. The <monotone coupling of Bernoulli percolation> shows that $\theta$ is a <monotone function> of $p$.

= Boundary-side amplification for planar percolation
{parent=Percolation probability}
{title2=$P(E_T\cap E_B)\ge1-2(1-P(D_N))^{1/4}$}

If an <infinite percolation cluster> exists almost surely, the <probability> that a large square box intersects one tends to one. The four rotationally symmetric exterior-side arm events are increasing. By the <Harris-FKG inequality>, their common <probability> is at least $1-(1-P(D_N))^{1/4}$, where $D_N$ is their union. Thus a pair of specified opposite sides both has an infinite exterior arm with <probability> at least $1-2(1-P(D_N))^{1/4}$, tending to one. The rays start at a boundary <graph vertex> and immediately leave the <graph vertex> box.

= Near-critical percolation power upper bound
{parent=Percolation probability}
{title2=$\theta(p)\leq A(p-1/2)^\beta$}

Suppose a planar <bond percolation> model has critical <one-arm probability> at most $Cn^{-\alpha}$ with $\alpha>0$, and a finite-radius connection event depends on $O(n^2)$ <edges>. The <finite-box comparison for percolation parameters> gives $\theta(p_c+\varepsilon)\leq Cn^{-\alpha}+C'\varepsilon n^2$. Balancing at $n\asymp\varepsilon^{-1/(\alpha+2)}$ gives $\theta(p_c+\varepsilon)\leq A\varepsilon^{\alpha/(\alpha+2)}$. For the <square lattice> use $p_c=1/2$ and the <polynomial critical one-arm upper bound>.

= Right continuity of percolation probability
{parent=Percolation probability}

For independent <site percolation> or <bond percolation> on a rooted <locally finite graph>, $\theta(p)$ is right continuous on $[0,1)$. Finite-radius connection events have polynomial <probabilities> in $p$ and decrease to infinite connection. Thus $\theta$ is their infimum by <continuity from above of a measure>. This infimum of <continuous functions> is upper semicontinuous; parameter monotonicity from the <monotone coupling of Bernoulli percolation> then gives right continuity.

= Supercritical continuity of percolation probability
{parent=Right continuity of percolation probability}

Independent <site percolation> and <bond percolation> on the <cubic lattice> have continuous <percolation probability> on $(p_c,1]$. Fix $p>p_c$ and an intermediate $q\in(p_c,p)$. By <uniqueness of the infinite percolation cluster>, every origin in the infinite $p$-cluster has a finite $p$-open <graph path> to the infinite $q$-cluster. In the uniform-label <monotone coupling of Bernoulli percolation>, the finitely many labels on this <graph path> are strictly below fixed $p$ <almost surely>, so it persists at some parameter below $p$. The <coupled onset parameter for origin percolation> has no attained atom there, giving left continuity, including at $1$. Combine this with <right continuity of percolation probability>.

= Percolation critical probability
{title2=$p_c$}
{parent=Percolation probability}

= Critical probability
{synonym}

For the rooted infinite model defining <percolation probability>, the percolation critical probability is
$$
p_c=\inf\{p:\theta(p)>0\}.
$$
Below $p_c$, the root has <probability> zero of belonging to an <infinite percolation cluster>. Above $p_c$, that <probability> is positive, by the definition and the <monotone coupling of Bernoulli percolation>. The definition alone does not determine behavior at $p_c$, or the numerical value of $p_c$: these require results specific to the <graph> and the model.

A finite <graph> with $M$ <edges> has $2^M$ open/closed configurations. The <probability> of any connectivity <event> is a finite sum of terms $p^k(1-p)^{M-k}$, hence a <polynomial> in $p$. The square's $2p^2-p^4$ is an example. These finite <probabilities> vary smoothly, although an infinite-system limit can distinguish a whole interval with $\theta(p)=0$ from one with $\theta(p)>0$. A steep finite connection curve and a mathematically proved <percolation critical probability> are different kinds of information.

= Sharpness of the percolation transition
{parent=Percolation critical probability}
{title2=$p<p_c\ \Longrightarrow\ \mathbb P_p(0\leftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_n)\leq Ce^{-cn}$}

= Percolation sharpness theorem
{synonym}

Independent <bond percolation> on the <cubic lattice> has exponential connection decay at every $p<p_c$ and positive <percolation probability> at every $p>p_c$. The <finite-set criterion for percolation sharpness> gives decay below its auxiliary threshold. The <Margulis–Russo formula> and exploration from a finite box boundary give $g_n'(p)\geq(1-g_n(p))\inf_{S\ni0}\varphi_p(S)/(p(1-p))$, with the infimum over interior finite sets. Above the auxiliary threshold the infimum is at least one; integrating gives positive <percolation probability> and identifies the two thresholds. This theorem does not identify the value of $p_c$ for a particular lattice.

= Connective-constant lower bound for percolation
{parent=Percolation critical probability}
{title2=$p_c\geq\mu^{-1}$}

For independent <bond percolation> on a transitive locally finite lattice, let $c_n$ count rooted <self-avoiding walks> and let $\mu=\lim c_n^{1/n}$ be the <connective constant>. An infinite open <percolation cluster> supplies an open walk of every length. The <union bound> gives $\theta(p)\leq c_np^n$, which tends to zero when $p\mu<1$. Thus $p_c\geq1/\mu$.

= Harris-Kesten theorem
{c}
{parent=Percolation critical probability}
{title2=$p_c(\mathbb Z^2)=1/2$}

The <percolation critical probability> of independent <bond percolation> on the infinite <square lattice> is exactly
$$
\boxed{p_c(\mathbb Z^2)=\frac12.}
$$
In this model, $\theta(p)=0$ for $p\leq1/2$ and $\theta(p)>0$ for $p>1/2$. The particular <graph>, independent <edge> decisions, and distinction between <bond percolation> and <site percolation> matter: this value is not a universal threshold for arbitrary random networks. The equality of open and closed <edge> <probabilities> at $1/2$ suggests a relation to <dual bond percolation>, but that symmetry alone is not a proof of long-distance connectivity.

Harris established the lower bound, including absence of an <infinite percolation cluster> at $p=1/2$, in 1960. Kesten completed the equality in 1980. His original paper states the threshold result as Theorem 1 and the positive/zero <percolation probability> alternatives as Theorem 2: https://math.bme.hu/~balint/oktatas/perkolacio/percolation_papers/kesten_1.pdf[Harry Kesten, The critical probability of bond percolation on the square lattice equals 1/2]{ref}. These are claims about the infinite <square lattice>, rather than deductions from a simulated finite sample.

= Harris theorem for square-lattice bond percolation
{c}
{parent=Harris-Kesten theorem}
{title2=$\theta(1/2)=0$}

Uniform fixed-aspect <rectangle crossing in bond percolation> bounds at the self-dual parameter yield a positive lower bound for closed dual circuits in geometrically spaced disjoint annuli. The <independent annular barriers for percolation> imply that an origin-to-infinity open <graph path> has probability zero. Parameter monotonicity gives zero <percolation probability> for every $p\leq1/2$.

= Alternating arms argument at the self-dual percolation parameter
{parent=Harris-Kesten theorem}

= Alternating arms at the self-dual parameter
{synonym}

= Zhang's argument
{synonym}
{c}

= Zhang argument
{synonym}
{c}

At parameter $1/2$, primal and <dual bond percolation> on the <square lattice> have the same <probability distribution>. Suppose an <infinite percolation cluster> exists. Large boxes meet it with <probability> tending to one. The <square-root trick for positively associated events> and quarter-turn symmetry imply that each side has an infinite exterior open arm with <probability> tending to one. The same holds for dual arms. A <union bound> then gives positive <probability> of primal top and bottom arms together with dual left and right arms.

Use a primal box $[-r,r]^2$ and a dual box with sides at $\pm(r+1/2)$. Open only primal <edges> whose endpoints both lie in the primal box, preserving all outward and exterior arm <edges>. The two primal arms are joined inside. Planarity prevents the two alternating dual arms from joining: a proposed dual connection together with the inner boundary arc separates one primal infinite arm from infinity. Thus the modified configuration has two infinite dual clusters. Its positive <probability> by <finite modification of Bernoulli percolation> contradicts <uniqueness of the infinite percolation cluster>. Consequently the <percolation probability> at $1/2$ is zero. This is often called Zhang's argument.

= Three-fold symmetric primal-dual noncoexistence
{parent=Alternating arms argument at the self-dual percolation parameter}

For independent <bond percolation> on a periodic plane lattice with three-fold rotation symmetry, the primal model at $p$ and the <dual bond percolation> model at $1-p$ cannot both percolate. To adapt <Zhang's argument>, divide a large boundary circle into six arcs, repeating the division in each $120$-degree sector. Vary the division point until the failure probabilities $a,b$ of the two alternating primal arc types are comparable within a factor $(1-p)^{-2}$. Moving past a single crossing edge changes a failure probability by at most a factor $(1-p)^{-1}$, by the <FKG inequality>. If the probability of no primal exterior arm is at most $\varepsilon$, then $a^3b^3\le\varepsilon$ and both failures are at most $(1-p)^{-1}\varepsilon^{1/6}$. For the dual model, at least one arc type has failure at most $\varepsilon^{1/6}$; its three rotations have the same bound. Four alternating primal/dual arms now occur with positive probability, contradicting the <alternating exterior-arm separation lemma> and uniqueness after a finite modification. This extends the four-fold argument to the <martini lattice>.

= Alternating exterior-arm separation lemma
{parent=Alternating arms argument at the self-dual percolation parameter}

Four infinite exterior arms entering a square in alternating primal-open and dual-open order force the two primal arms to be disjoint outside the square. If they met, their finite connecting crosscut together with a boundary arc would enclose one dual arm, preventing its escape to infinity. Connecting the two primal arms through the square creates a proper doubly infinite <open path>, separating the two remaining dual infinite tails. <finite-energy property of Bernoulli percolation> can then turn this configuration into two infinite dual clusters, contradicting uniqueness. Care is needed to preserve the exterior dual tails even if the finite modification destroys their original boundary attachments.

= Monotone coupling of Bernoulli percolation
{parent=Percolation theory}

Assign each <edge> $e$ an <independent> <uniform distribution> label $U_e$ on $[0,1]$. At parameter $p$, declare $e$ open exactly when $U_e\leq p$. Each open/closed decision then has the required <Bernoulli distribution>, and different <edge> decisions are <independent>. The same construction applies to <site percolation> with labels on <vertices>.

Keep those labels fixed when increasing $p$. For $p\leq q$, every $p$-open <edge> is also $q$-open, so every $p$-open <graph path> survives and each <percolation cluster> can only gain <vertices>. This <coupling of probability distributions> compares complete configurations on one <probability space>, rather than comparing two unrelated pictures. Any <increasing event>, such as an <open path> between specified <vertices>, consequently has a nondecreasing <probability>.

\Image[percolation-monotone-grid.png]
{title=One set of random edge labels gives nested root clusters as the opening probability increases}

The three panels use one seeded collection of labels on a finite $17\times17$ <square lattice> box. Colored <edges> and dots show the central root's <percolation cluster>; gray <edges> are open but disconnected from it. A <percolation cluster> can grow by many <vertices> when one newly opened <edge> joins it to another large <percolation cluster>. The panels illustrate a nested construction, not an estimate of $\theta(p)$ or a proof of the <Harris-Kesten theorem>.

= Finite-box comparison for percolation parameters
{parent=Monotone coupling of Bernoulli percolation}
{title2=$\mathbb P_q(A)-\mathbb P_p(A)\leq m(q-p)$}

If an <increasing event> $A$ depends on $m$ sites or <edges>, the uniform-label <monotone coupling of Bernoulli percolation> and the <union bound> give $0\leq\mathbb P_q(A)-\mathbb P_p(A)\leq m(q-p)$ for $p\leq q$. A change of the event requires a coordinate with label in $(p,q]$. For <bond percolation> from the origin to the boundary of $[-n,n]^2$, first-hit <graph paths> use the $4n(2n+1)$ internal <edges>, so the same count bounds the parameter sensitivity.

= Coupled onset parameter for origin percolation
{parent=Monotone coupling of Bernoulli percolation}
{title2=$M=\inf\{p:I_p\}$}

In the uniform-label <monotone coupling of Bernoulli percolation>, let $I_p$ mean that the origin belongs to an <infinite percolation cluster>, and let $M=\inf\{p:I_p\}$. The occurrence parameters form an upper interval. Therefore $\{M<p\}=\bigcup_{r<p,\ r\in\mathbb Q}I_r$ and $\theta(p-)=\mathbb P(M<p)$. Since $\{M<p\}\subseteq I_p\subseteq\{M\leq p\}$, the jump is $\theta(p)-\theta(p-)=\mathbb P(I_p\cap\{M=p\})$. Occurrence at the infimum is not presumed.

= Sprinkling coupling for Bernoulli percolation
{parent=Monotone coupling of Bernoulli percolation}

For $p<q$, first sample Bernoulli percolation at parameter $p$, then independently open each remaining closed coordinate with probability $(q-p)/(1-p)$. Every coordinate is then open with probability $q$, so the union has the parameter-$q$ law. This coupling separates existing $p$-clusters from the additional sprinkled connections between them.

\Include[site-percolation]{parent=Percolation theory}

= Peierls argument
{c}
{parent=Percolation theory}
{wiki}

A Peierls argument bounds a rare large defect by encoding it as a contour, estimating the number of contours of each size, and summing their exponentially small probabilities.

= Connective-constant Peierls bound
{parent=Peierls argument}
{title2=$p_c\leq1-\mu^{-1}$}

Independent <bond percolation> on the <square lattice> satisfies $p_c\leq1-\mu^{-1}$, where $\mu$ is its <connective constant>. If $(1-p)\mu<1$, closed dual <graph cycles> have a summable large-length tail: their counts are bounded by a polynomial factor times the count of <self-avoiding walks>. Conditioning a sufficiently large finite box to be open excludes short enclosing <graph cycles> without changing the law of the remaining <edges>. With positive conditional <probability> no enclosing closed dual <graph cycle> remains, so the origin belongs to an <infinite percolation cluster>.

= Finite-energy property
{disambiguate=percolation}
{parent=Percolation theory}

= Finite-energy property
{synonym}

A <percolation> measure has the finite-energy property if, conditional on all exterior states, every prescribed state of any finite collection of sites or edges has positive <probability> almost surely. This permits finite local modifications within events of positive probability. It is a hypothesis on the measure and does not follow merely from invariance under graph symmetries.

= Finite-energy property of Bernoulli percolation
{parent=Finite-energy property (percolation)}

For $0<p<1$, prescribing any finite collection of Bernoulli percolation sites or edges has positive conditional probability given all states outside that collection.

= Finite modification of Bernoulli percolation
{parent=Finite-energy property of Bernoulli percolation}

For $0<p<1$, every prescribed state pattern on a finite <edge> <set> has positive conditional <probability> given all exterior states: <independence> makes it $p^a(1-p)^b$ for $a$ prescribed open and $b$ prescribed closed <edges>. Thus a positive-probability exterior event remains possible after any finite local surgery. This is the <finite-energy property of Bernoulli percolation>, and applies equally to <dual bond percolation>.

= Exponential decay of subcritical percolation
{parent=Percolation theory}

Below the percolation threshold, the probability that a fixed vertex has an open path to graph distance $n$ is at most $Ce^{-cn}$ for positive constants $C,c$.

= Finite-set criterion for percolation sharpness
{parent=Exponential decay of subcritical percolation}
{title2=$\varphi_p(S)=p\sum_{x\in S,\ y\notin S,\ x\sim y}\mathbb P_p(0\leftrightarrow x\text{ in }S)$}

For a finite vertex set $S$ containing the origin, this quantity is the expected number of potentially open exit edges reached from the origin within $S$. If $\varphi_p(S)<1$, the <BK inequality> applied to the first exit of a long open <self-avoiding walk> gives a contraction at the scale of $S$ and hence exponential connection decay. The threshold $\widetilde p_c=\sup\{p:\varphi_p(S)<1\text{ for some finite }S\ni0\}$ equals the <percolation critical probability> by the pivotal-edge differential inequality in <sharpness of the percolation transition>.

= Exponential tail of subcritical cluster size
{parent=Exponential decay of subcritical percolation}

For independent <bond percolation> on $\mathbb Z^d$ below the <percolation critical probability>, there are $K,c>0$ such that $\mathbb P_p(|C(x)|\geq n)\leq Ke^{-cn}$, uniformly in $x$. This concerns the number of vertices in a <percolation cluster>, a stronger conclusion than decay of its radius alone.

= One-arm event
{title2=$A_R$}
{parent=Percolation theory}

The one-arm event $A_R$ is the event that a specified root is joined through open vertices or edges to graph distance $R$.

= OSSS inequality
{c}
{parent=Percolation theory}

For a Boolean function of independent coordinates and a randomized <decision tree> that determines it, the OSSS inequality bounds its variance by a sum of coordinate influences weighted by their revealment probabilities.

= OSSS exploration of a one-arm event
{c}
{parent=OSSS inequality}

Choosing an intermediate radius uniformly and exploring the open cluster meeting that sphere gives a decision tree for a <one-arm event>. Translation and a union bound control each revealment by a constant times the average of the one-arm probabilities over the possible radii.

= Finitely additive probability measure
{parent=Probability theory}

A finitely additive probability measure on a set $X$ is a function $m:\mathcal P(X)\to[0,1]$ such that $m(X)=1$ and $m(A\sqcup B)=m(A)+m(B)$ for disjoint subsets $A,B\subseteq X$. Unlike a <probability measure>, it need not be countably additive.

= Probability space
{title2=$(\Omega,\mathcal F,\mathbb P)$}
{parent=Probability theory}
{wiki}

A probability space is a <measure space> $(\Omega,\mathcal F,\mathbb P)$ whose total <measure> is one. The <sample space> $\Omega$ contains the elementary outcomes; the <sigma-algebra> $\mathcal F$ contains the <events> to which the model assigns <probabilities>; the <probability measure> $\mathbb P$ supplies those values. The <sigma-algebra> lets us combine observable questions using <complements> and countable <unions> while remaining within the model.

For two independent uniform dice, take $\Omega=\{1,\ldots,6\}^2$, $\mathcal F=\mathcal P(\Omega)$, and give each ordered pair mass $1/36$. A <random variable> might record the sum, the larger result, or whether both results are <even integers>. These are different <functions> on the same <sample space>, rather than different elementary experiments. A <probability distribution> records the mass of the values of one such <random variable> and can forget information retained by the full <probability space>.

= Product probability space
{parent=Probability space}
{title2=$(\prod_iX_i,\bigotimes_i\mathcal F_i,\bigotimes_i\mu_i)$}

For finitely many <probability spaces> $(X_i,\mathcal F_i,\mu_i)$, their product <probability> space has underlying <set> $\prod_iX_i$, the <product sigma-algebra> and <product measure> $\bigotimes_i\mu_i$. The coordinate projections are jointly independent: for measurable $B_i\subseteq X_i$, the <probability> of all coordinate conditions is $\prod_i\mu_i(B_i)$. Conversely a family of independent coordinates has this product joint distribution. Product structure permits coordinate exposure and independent resampling in concentration arguments.

= Sample space
{parent=Probability space}
{title2=$\Omega$}

The <sample space> is the set $\Omega$ of possible outcomes underlying a <probability space> $(\Omega,\mathcal F,\mathbb P)$. Its measurable subsets are <events>, and the whole <sample space> has probability one.

= Almost sure event
{parent=Probability space}
{title2=$\mathbb P(A)=1$}

An almost sure event is a measurable event of probability one. Countable intersections of such events still have probability one, allowing a conclusion to hold simultaneously for countably many parameter choices. Further deterministic reasoning may then extend it to uncountably many parameters, as in <Brownian Hölder regularity> or <nowhere monotonicity of Brownian motion>. An uncountable intersection need not preserve probability one without such additional reasoning.

= Probability measure
{parent=Probability space}
{wiki}

A probability measure is a nonnegative, <countably additive> <measure> with total mass one. Thus $\mathbb P(\Omega)=1$, and for pairwise <disjoint events> $A_j$,
$$
\mathbb P\left(\bigcup_{j=1}^{\infty}A_j\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathbb P(A_j).
$$
The <complement of an event> has <probability> $1-\mathbb P(A)$. The <probability> of a <union> of overlapping <events> needs a correction: $\mathbb P(A\cup B)=\mathbb P(A)+\mathbb P(B)-\mathbb P(A\cap B)$. Counting the overlap once is the simplest instance of the <inclusion-exclusion principle>.

A <zero-probability event> need not be impossible as a member of the <sample space>. Under the <continuous uniform distribution> on $[0,1]$, every individual point has <probability> zero, while the whole interval has <probability> one. <Countable additivity> applies to countable <unions>, not arbitrary uncountable ones; there is no contradiction.

= Continuity of probability
{parent=Probability measure}
{title2=$A_n\uparrow A\text{ or }A_n\downarrow A\ \Longrightarrow\ \mathbb P(A_n)\to\mathbb P(A)$}

<Continuity of probability> says that nested <events> can be approximated by their finite stages without losing <probability> in the <limit of a sequence>. In a <probability space> $(\Omega,\mathcal F,\mathbb P)$, let $A_n,B_n\in\mathcal F$. Then
$$
\begin{aligned}
A_1\subseteq A_2\subseteq\cdots,\quad A=\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}A_n
&\quad\Longrightarrow\quad \mathbb P(A_n)\uparrow\mathbb P(A),\\
B_1\supseteq B_2\supseteq\cdots,\quad B=\bigcap_{n=1}^{\infty}B_n
&\quad\Longrightarrow\quad \mathbb P(B_n)\downarrow\mathbb P(B).
\end{aligned}
$$
The <sigma-algebra> ensures that both limiting <events> belong to $\mathcal F$. These are <continuity from below of a measure> and <continuity from above of a measure>. The first holds even for an infinite <measure>; the second requires finite <measure> of the first <set>, which is automatic for a <probability measure> because $\mathbb P(B_1)\leq1$.<Probability 1 lecture notes, Chapter 1: Axioms of probability by Durham Statistics and Probability teaching team>{ref}

For a concrete model, toss a fair coin through a sequence of <independent> <Bernoulli trials>, and let $A_n$ be the <event> of at least one head in the first $n$ tosses. Its <complement> $B_n=A_n^c$ is the <event> of $n$ initial tails. On the <sample space> of infinite head-or-tail sequences,
$$
\mathbb P(A_n)=1-2^{-n},\qquad \mathbb P(B_n)=2^{-n},\qquad
\mathbb P\left(\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}A_n\right)=1,\qquad
\mathbb P\left(\bigcap_{n=1}^{\infty}B_n\right)=0.
$$
Every finite waiting horizon has positive <tail probability>, yet the <event> of waiting forever has <probability> zero. That <intersection> is not empty: it contains the all-tails sequence. Thus a <zero-probability event> need not be impossible as an outcome, and eventual success <almost surely> does not supply a deterministic deadline. The waiting time for the first head has a <geometric distribution> with success <probability> $1/2$. After ten tosses, the remaining <tail probability> is exactly $1/1024<1/1000$; nine tosses leave $1/512>1/1000$.

\Image[probability-axioms-continuity-nested-limits.png]{title=Exact fair-coin model: success by toss n increases to probability one, while the all-tails prefix probability decreases to zero}

A proof outline exposes what makes <countable additivity> stronger than <finite additivity of a set function>. For increasing <events>, put $D_1=A_1$ and $D_n=A_n\setminus A_{n-1}$ for $n\geq2$. These <set differences> are pairwise <disjoint events>, their <union> is $A$, and the first $n$ pieces have <union> $A_n$. Consequently
$$
\mathbb P(A)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathbb P(D_j)
=\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb P(D_j)
=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb P(A_n).
$$
For decreasing <events>, their <complements> increase to $B^c$; applying the increasing result and the <complement rule of probability> gives $\mathbb P(B_n)=1-\mathbb P(B_n^c)\to1-\mathbb P(B^c)=\mathbb P(B)$. The finite-total-mass condition is essential for this subtraction. For comparison, the <counting measure> of each <set> $\{n,n+1,\ldots\}$ of <natural numbers> is infinite, although their <intersection> is empty and has <measure> zero.

Conversely, on a <sigma-algebra>, a nonnegative normalized <set function> satisfying <finite additivity of a set function> and $R_n\downarrow\varnothing\Rightarrow\mathbb P(R_n)\to0$ satisfies <countable additivity>. For pairwise <disjoint events> $D_j$ with $E=\bigcup_jD_j$, the remainders $R_n=E\setminus\bigcup_{j=1}^nD_j$ decrease to the empty <set>, and
$$
\mathbb P(E)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb P(D_j)+\mathbb P(R_n).
$$
Taking the <limit of a sequence> recovers the infinite sum. This equivalence explains why <continuity of probability> is part of the foundations rather than a separate assumption about smooth curves.

One useful application controls infinitely many possible errors. If <events> $E_j$ satisfy $\sum_j\mathbb P(E_j)<\infty$, then the <events> $C_n=\bigcup_{j\geq n}E_j$ decrease, and the <union bound> gives $\mathbb P(C_n)\leq\sum_{j\geq n}\mathbb P(E_j)\to0$. <Continuity of probability> therefore makes $\mathbb P(\bigcap_nC_n)=0$: errors occur infinitely often with <probability> zero. This is the <Borel-Cantelli first lemma>, and it requires no <independence>. For the illustrative bounds $\mathbb P(E_j)\leq2^{-j}$, the <probability> of any error at index $n$ or later is at most $2^{1-n}$, an explicit truncation guarantee.

The word continuity here concerns nested <sets>, not the absence of atoms in a <probability distribution>. For any real-valued <random variable> $X$, the <events> $\{X\leq x+1/n\}$ decrease to $\{X\leq x\}$. Hence its <cumulative distribution function> is <right-continuous> even when it has jumps, as for a <geometric distribution>. The nesting hypothesis matters: an arbitrary sequence of <events> need not admit either displayed interchange of <limit> and <probability>.

= Tightness of a probability measure
{parent=Probability measure}
{title2=$\forall\varepsilon>0\ \exists K\text{ compact}:\mu(K)>1-\varepsilon$}

A <probability measure> is tight if compact sets carry arbitrarily nearly all its mass. Every <Borel probability measure> on a <Polish space> is tight. The stronger family condition is <uniform tightness>.

= Aldous tightness criterion
{c}
{parent=Tightness of a probability measure}

For càdlàg processes on a finite horizon, compact containment together with uniformly small increments over pairs of bounded <stopping times> whose separation tends to zero is a sufficient criterion for tightness in the <Skorokhod J1 topology>. The increment condition is in probability, not merely at deterministic times. If in addition the largest jump tends to zero in probability, every limiting law is supported on continuous paths.

= Uniform tightness
{parent=Tightness of a probability measure}
{title2=$\forall\varepsilon>0\ \exists K\text{ compact}:\inf_{\mu\in S}\mu(K)>1-\varepsilon$}

= Uniformly tight
{synonym}

= Tightness of probability measures
{synonym}

The same <compact set> must work simultaneously for every <measure> in the family. <Prokhorov's theorem> relates this condition to relative compactness for the <weak topology of probability measures> on a <Polish space>.

= Helly selection for distribution functions
{c}
{parent=Uniform tightness}

A tight sequence of <probability measures> on the real line has a subsequence whose <cumulative distribution functions> converge at every continuity point of a <cumulative distribution function>. Choose a diagonal subsequence converging at every rational $q$, write those limits as $f(q)$, and define $F(x)=\inf_{q>x}f(q)$. Monotonicity gives right continuity, and <tightness of probability measures> gives limits zero and one at the two ends of the real line. Bracketing by rational continuity approximations proves convergence at the continuity points of $F$. The corresponding <probability measure> is the weak subsequential limit: approximate a bounded continuous test function by step functions on a compact interval, and use tightness to bound the tails.

= Zero-probability event
{parent=Probability measure}
{title2=$\mathbb P(A)=0$}

A zero-probability event has <probability measure> zero. It need not be empty: for a continuously distributed <random variable>, equality with a specified value is such an event. The complementary event occurs <almost surely>.

= Empirical measure
{parent=Probability measure}
{title2=$\nu_N=\frac1N\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\delta_{x_j}$}

For observations or orbit points $x_0,\ldots,x_{N-1}$, the empirical measure is $\nu_N=N^{-1}\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\delta_{x_j}$. Integrating a test function gives its sample average. For orbit points $x_j=T^jx$, the difference between $T_*\nu_N$ and $\nu_N$ telescopes to $(\delta_{T^Nx}-\delta_x)/N$, so on a compact <metric space> with continuous $T$, every weak limit is invariant.

= Compactly supported continuous functions determine a finite Borel measure
{parent=Probability measure}

If finite <Borel measures> $\mu$ and $\nu$ on $\mathbb R$ satisfy
$$
\int f\,d\mu=\int f\,d\nu
$$
for every compactly supported <continuous function> $f$, then $\mu=\nu$. This follows from the uniqueness part of the <Riesz representation theorem>; alternatively, continuous functions that approximate indicators from above and below recover the measure of every interval and hence every Borel set.

= Kolmogorov axioms
{c}
{parent=Probability measure}

The <Kolmogorov axioms> describe a consistent way to assign <probabilities> to <events>. Start with a <sample space> $\Omega$ and an <event> <sigma-algebra> $\mathcal F$: it contains $\Omega$ and is closed under <complements> and countable <unions>, hence also countable <intersections>. The <probability measure> is a <function> $\mathbb P:\mathcal F\to\mathbb R$ satisfying
$$
\begin{aligned}
&\mathbb P(A)\geq0 &&\text{for every }A\in\mathcal F,\\
&\mathbb P(\Omega)=1,\\
&\mathbb P\left(\bigcup_{j=1}^{\infty}A_j\right)
=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathbb P(A_j)
&&\text{if }A_i\cap A_j=\varnothing\text{ for }i\ne j.
\end{aligned}
$$
The triple $(\Omega,\mathcal F,\mathbb P)$ is a <probability space>. The third axiom is <countable additivity>; the infinite <series> means the <limit of a sequence> of nonnegative partial sums. It applies to pairwise <disjoint events>, not to overlapping <events> and not to arbitrary uncountable <unions>.<Introduction to Probability, Lecture 1: Probability Models and Axioms by John Tsitsiklis and others>{ref}

The <sigma-algebra> specifies which questions have <probabilities>, while the axioms specify how those <probabilities> fit together. For a finite <sample space> one can take the entire <power set>; a continuous model commonly uses a <Borel sigma-algebra>. Nonnegativity and normalization do not require equally likely outcomes or <independence>: those are additional modelling choices.

For an exact six-outcome model, take $\Omega=\{1,2,3,4,5,6\}$, $\mathcal F=\mathcal P(\Omega)$, and assign $\mathbb P(\{k\})=k/21$. The total is one because $1+2+3+4+5+6=21$. Define the <probability> of an <event> by summing the weights of its outcomes. The <disjoint events> $A=\{1,2\}$, $B=\{3,4\}$ and $C=\{5,6\}$ form a <partition of a set> with
$$
\mathbb P(A)=\frac3{21},\qquad \mathbb P(B)=\frac7{21},\qquad
\mathbb P(C)=\frac{11}{21},\qquad
\mathbb P(A\cup B)=\frac{10}{21}=1-\mathbb P(C).
$$
This is a specified mathematical model, not measured die frequencies. The figure makes <probability> literal area: each region has the same height and a width proportional to its assigned mass. Regrouping <disjoint events> preserves their total area.

\Image[probability-axioms-continuity-weighted-partition.png]{title=Exact six-outcome model with masses k/21: grouping disjoint outcomes adds their probability areas}

Several familiar rules are consequences, not further axioms. <Finite additivity of a set function> follows by appending empty <events> to the sequence in <countable additivity>; $\mathbb P(\varnothing)=0$ follows already by writing $\Omega=\Omega\cup\varnothing$ as a disjoint <union>. The <complement rule of probability> gives $\mathbb P(A^c)=1-\mathbb P(A)$ and therefore $0\leq\mathbb P(A)\leq1$. Splitting larger <events> into a smaller <event> and a <set difference> shows $A\subseteq B\Rightarrow\mathbb P(A)\leq\mathbb P(B)$.

For overlapping <events>, the <inclusion-exclusion principle> corrects double counting:
$$
\mathbb P(D\cup E)=\mathbb P(D)+\mathbb P(E)-\mathbb P(D\cap E).
$$
In the six-outcome model, $D=\{2,3\}$ and $E=\{3,4\}$ give $5/21+7/21-3/21=9/21$, agreeing with the weight of $\{2,3,4\}$. This decomposition is useful because a complicated <event> can be assembled from simpler questions with known <probabilities>.

On a finite <sample space>, a family of pairwise <disjoint events> has only finitely many nonempty members, so <finite additivity of a set function> already handles every countable disjoint <union>. On an infinite <sample space>, the countable axiom additionally controls what is left beyond every finite stage. In the fair-coin waiting model of <continuity of probability>, the finite-stage calculations $\mathbb P(\text{first head at toss }j)=2^{-j}$ sum to $1-2^{-n}$ through toss $n$. <Countable additivity> makes the <probability> of a head at some finite toss equal to $\sum_{j\geq1}2^{-j}=1$; finite-stage sums alone do not justify that passage. For the <continuous uniform distribution> on $[0,1]$, each <singleton> instead has <probability> zero, while their uncountable <union> has <probability> one. Countable, rather than unrestricted, additivity accommodates both models.

Historically, Kolmogorov's 1933 book \i[<Grundbegriffe der Wahrscheinlichkeitsrechnung>] established the influential axiomatic framework connecting <probability theory> with <measure theory>. The three conditions above are the modern presentation on a <sigma-algebra>. Shafer and Vovk's historical study explains that the original presentation began with finite additivity on an <algebra of sets> and added continuity for decreasing <events> with empty <intersection>, which supplies the countable extension.<The Sources of Kolmogorov’s Grundbegriffe by Glenn Shafer and others>{ref}

= Law of total probability
{parent=Probability measure}
{wiki}

Let $(B_i)$ be a countable <measurable partition> of the <sample space>, with positive <probabilities>. For any <event> $A$, the law of total probability gives
$$
\mathbb P(A)=\sum_i\mathbb P(A\mid B_i)\mathbb P(B_i).
$$
The pieces $A\cap B_i$ are <disjoint events>, so add their masses and use the definition of <conditional probability>. Zero-mass partition pieces can be omitted without changing the answer.

For the coin selection in <probability>, let $B$ mean the $3/4$-head coin was selected and let $H$ mean the first toss was heads. Then $\mathbb P(H)=\mathbb P(H\mid B)\mathbb P(B)+\mathbb P(H\mid B^c)\mathbb P(B^c)=11/20$. Each conditional answer describes one possible hidden situation; its weight describes how often that situation occurs under the model. This decomposition is useful whenever a simple conditional model sits behind a more complicated marginal <probability distribution>.

= Event
{parent=Probability space}
{wiki=Event_(probability_theory)}

An event is a <measurable set> in a <probability space>: a question with a yes-or-no answer for each outcome. On $\Omega=\{1,\ldots,6\}^2$, the <event> that the sum is at least nine consists of ten ordered pairs. Its <probability> is therefore $10/36=5/18$ under independent uniform dice. Saying that an <event> occurred means the actual outcome belongs to that <set>.

= Limsup of events
{parent=Event}
{title2=$\limsup_{n\to\infty}A_n$}

= Infinitely often event
{synonym}

For <events> $A_n$ in a <probability space>, the infinitely-often <event> is
$$
\limsup_{n\to\infty}A_n
=\bigcap_{m=1}^{\infty}\bigcup_{n=m}^{\infty}A_n
=\{\omega:\text{for every }m\text{ there is }n\geq m\text{ with }\omega\in A_n\}.
$$
Its <complement of an event> is $\bigcup_{m\geq1}\bigcap_{n\geq m}A_n^c$: after some outcome-dependent index, no more <events> occur.<Probability: Theory and Examples (fifth edition) by Rick Durrett>[§2.3 opening definition and infinitely-often notation, printed pp. 67–68]{ref}

Deleting finitely many <events> leaves the <limsup of events> unchanged. It is therefore a <tail event> of the sequence of <indicator random variables> $\mathbf1_{A_n}$, whether or not those <random variables> are <independent>. For <independent events>, the <Kolmogorov zero-one law> restricts its <probability> to zero or one; the <Borel-Cantelli lemmas> determine which alternative holds from the <series> of <probabilities>. Also $\mathbf1_{A_n}\to0$ precisely when only finitely many <events> occur, connecting the <first Borel-Cantelli lemma> to <almost sure convergence> and the existing <complete convergence> criterion.

= Complement of an event
{parent=Event}
{title2=$A^c=\Omega\setminus A$}

The <event> that $A$ does not occur has probability $1-P(A)$. Conditioning on an <event> positively associated with $A$ negatively associates its complement with $A$.

= Disjoint events
{parent=Event}
{title2=$E\cap F=\varnothing$}

= Mutually exclusive events
{synonym}

Two <events> are disjoint when they cannot both occur. For a finite family of pairwise disjoint events, the probability of their union is the sum of their probabilities, so that sum is at most one. This condition differs from the condition defining <independent events>.

= Cylinder sigma-algebra
{title2=$\mathcal C$}
{parent=Probability space}

= Coordinate sigma-algebra
{synonym}

On a product space $\mathbb R^T$, the cylinder sigma-algebra is generated by coordinate projections. Every event in it depends on at most countably many coordinates.

= Uniform random point in a disk
{parent=Probability theory}

For a point uniformly distributed in the unit disk, its polar coordinates are independent with densities
$$
f_R(r)=2r\mathbf1_{[0,1]}(r),
\qquad
f_\Theta(\theta)=\frac1{2\pi}\mathbf1_{[0,2\pi)}(\theta).
$$

= Origin in a triangle of three radial random points
{parent=Uniform random point in a disk}

For three independent points from any rotationally symmetric planar distribution with nonzero radii, the origin lies in their triangle exactly when their three angles do not all lie in one semicircle. Three independent uniform angles have this property with probability $1/4$.

= Independent sigma-algebras
{parent=Probability theory}

Two sub-sigma-algebras $\mathcal F_1,\mathcal F_2$ are independent when
$$
\mathbb P(A\cap B)=\mathbb P(A)\mathbb P(B)
$$
for every $A\in\mathcal F_1$ and $B\in\mathcal F_2$.

= Independent events
{parent=Independent sigma-algebras}

Events $A_1,\ldots,A_n$ are mutually independent when
$$
\mathbb P\left(\bigcap_{j\in J}A_j\right)
=\prod_{j\in J}\mathbb P(A_j)
$$
for every nonempty index subset $J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}$. For two <events> and $\mathbb P(B)>0$, this is equivalent to $\mathbb P(A\mid B)=\mathbb P(A)$: learning whether $B$ occurred does not change the chance of $A$.

For independent uniform dice, the <events> that the first result is an <even integer> and that the second result is an <even integer> each have <probability> $1/2$; their <intersection> has <probability> $9/36=1/4$. In contrast, "first result is an <even integer>" and "first result is an <odd integer>" are <disjoint events> of positive <probability>, so they cannot be <independent events>. Learning one occurred completely rules out the other.

<Pairwise independence> is weaker than mutual independence. Take two <independent> fair <bits> $X,Y$. The three <events> $X=0$, $Y=0$, and $X=Y$ each have <probability> $1/2$ and every pair has <intersection> <probability> $1/4$. Their triple <intersection> is the single outcome $(0,0)$, of <probability> $1/4$ rather than $1/8$. Checking pairs alone misses the joint constraint.

= Pairwise-independent three-event avoidance
{parent=Independent events}
{title2=$\mathbb P(\bigcap_{i=1}^3A_i^c)\geq(1-a)(1-2a)$}

For three pairwise <independent events> of common probability $a$, write $x=\mathbb P(A_1\cap A_2\cap A_3)$. <Inclusion-exclusion principle> gives avoidance probability $1-3a+3a^2-x$, and $x\leq a^2$ gives the displayed lower bound. It is strictly positive for $0<a<1/2$. For $1/2\leq a<1$, a symmetric atomic law with no zero-success atom has mass $(1-a)^2$ on each singleton success pattern, $(1-a)(2a-1)$ on each doubleton, and $1-3a+3a^2$ on the tripleton. These masses sum to one, have marginal $a$, and have pair intersections $a^2$.

= Independence extended from generating pi-systems
{parent=Independent sigma-algebras}

If two pi-systems satisfy the probability factorization pairwise, their generated sigma-algebras are independent. For one event in the first family, the events in the second sigma-algebra satisfying the factorization form a Dynkin system; apply <Dynkin lemma>, then repeat with the roles reversed.

= Tail sigma-algebra
{title2=$\mathcal T$}
{parent=Independent sigma-algebras}

For a sequence of random variables $(X_n)$, the tail sigma-algebra is
$$
\mathcal T=\bigcap_{n\geq1}\sigma(X_n,X_{n+1},\ldots).
$$
Its events are unaffected by changing any finite initial segment.

= Tail measurability of limits of sample averages
{parent=Tail sigma-algebra}

For a sequence of finite real <random variables>, $\limsup_n n^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^nX_j$ is measurable with respect to its <tail sigma-algebra>: deleting finitely many summands changes each <sample mean> by a quantity tending to zero. When the <random variables> are <independent random variables>, the <Kolmogorov zero-one law> makes every finite such limit constant <almost surely>.

= Tail event
{parent=Tail sigma-algebra}
{wiki}

A tail event is measurable with respect to a <tail sigma-algebra>, so changing finitely many coordinates does not affect whether it occurs.

= Kolmogorov's zero–one law
{c}
{parent=Tail sigma-algebra}
{wiki}

= Kolmogorov zero-one law
{synonym}
{c}

The <tail sigma-algebra> of a sequence of <independent random variables> is trivial: every tail event has probability zero or one.

= Product measure
{parent=Probability theory}
{wiki}

The product measure $\bigotimes_n\mu_n$ is characterized on measurable rectangles depending on finitely many coordinates by the product of their component measures.

= Product sigma-algebra
{title2=$\mathcal A_1\otimes\mathcal A_2$}
{parent=Product measure}

The product sigma-algebra on $X_1\times X_2$ is generated by the measurable rectangles $A_1\times A_2$. It is the smallest sigma-algebra making both coordinate projections measurable.

= Pi-lambda theorem
{title2=$\pi$-$\lambda$ theorem}
{parent=Product measure}

If a <Dynkin system> contains a <pi-system>, then it contains the sigma-algebra generated by that pi-system. This proves uniqueness of finite measures from agreement on a generating pi-system.

= Canonical model of an independent and identically distributed sequence
{parent=Product measure}

= Canonical model of an iid sequence
{synonym}

For a probability law $\nu$ on $E$, take the sequence space $E^{\mathbb N}$ with <product measure> $\nu^{\otimes\mathbb N}$. The coordinate maps are an <independent and identically distributed random variables>[independent and identically distributed sequence] with common law $\nu$, and they generate the product <sigma-algebra>.

= Fréchet inequalities
{c}
{parent=Probability theory}

For events $A$ and $B$, the Fréchet inequalities are
$$
\max\{0,\mathbb P(A)+\mathbb P(B)-1\}
\leq\mathbb P(A\cap B)
\leq\min\{\mathbb P(A),\mathbb P(B)\}.
$$

= Standard score
{parent=Probability theory}
{title2=$z=(x-\mu)/\sigma$}
{wiki}

= Z-score
{c}
{synonym}

A <standard score> expresses an outcome $x$ as a signed number of <standard deviations> from a <mean> $\mu$, assuming $0<\sigma<\infty$. It is a <dimensionless quantity>. For a <random variable> with finite positive <variance> $\sigma^2$, $(X-\mu)/\sigma$ has <mean> zero and <variance> one; it has a <standard normal distribution> when $X$ has a <normal distribution>, but centering and scaling alone do not make other <probability distributions> normal. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-05-introduction-to-probability-and-statistics-spring-2022/mit18_05_s22_class06-prep-b.pdf[MIT 18.05, Reading 6b, §5.1: standardization]{ref}.

\Include[random-variable]{parent=Probability theory}
\Include[queueing-theory]{parent=Probability theory}
\Include[convergence-of-random-variables]{parent=Probability theory}

\Include[martingale]{parent=Probability theory}

= Borel-Cantelli lemmas
{c}
{parent=Probability theory}

= Borel-Cantelli lemma
{c}
{synonym}

The <Borel-Cantelli lemmas> turn a <series> of <event> <probabilities> into a statement about an entire infinite sequence of observations. A <summable sequence> of <probabilities> forces only finitely many occurrences <almost surely>, regardless of <independence>. A divergent <series> forces infinitely many occurrences when the <events> are mutually <independent events>. The precise <event> being measured is the <limsup of events>.

Imagine alarms with <probabilities> $p_n=1/(n+1)^2$ or $p_n=1/(n+1)$, starting at $n=1$. Both individual <probabilities> tend to zero. Nevertheless, the first alarm sequence eventually stops <almost surely>, whereas the second keeps returning <almost surely> if its <events> are mutually <independent events>. Reusing one <uniform random variable> for every alarm instead produces the <shared-uniform counterexample to the second Borel-Cantelli lemma>. Thus the individual <probabilities> alone do not determine long-run recurrence.

= Summable and harmonic event probabilities
{parent=Borel-Cantelli lemmas}

Take <independent random variables> $U_n$, each with the <continuous uniform distribution> on $[0,1]$, and set $A_n=\{U_n\leq p_n\}$. Then $\mathbb P(A_n)=p_n$, and the <events> are mutually <independent events>. The <product probability space> supplies a model for the entire infinite sequence; a finite list of simulated values is only an illustration.

For $p_n=1/(n+1)^2$, the <p-series> is <summable>:
$$
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}p_n=\frac{\pi^2}{6}-1,\qquad
\sum_{n=m}^{\infty}p_n\leq\int_m^{\infty}\frac{dx}{x^2}=\frac1m.
$$
The <Basel problem> supplies the total sum, and the <integral test for convergence> gives the displayed tail bound. The <first Borel-Cantelli lemma> makes the <limsup of events> have <probability> zero. In fact, the tail bound implies that the <probability> of even one alarm at or after $m$ is at most $1/m$; for $m=100$, it is at most $0.01$. This bound remains valid for dependent <events> with the same individual <probabilities>.

For $p_n=1/(n+1)$, the <harmonic series> diverges, so the <second Borel-Cantelli lemma> makes the <limsup of events> have <probability> one. There is also an exact finite-horizon calculation using <independent events>:
$$
\mathbb P\left(\bigcap_{n=m}^{M}A_n^c\right)
=\prod_{n=m}^{M}\frac{n}{n+1}=\frac{m}{M+1}.
$$
For any fixed $m$, this finite <product of numbers> tends to zero as $M\to\infty$. Every infinite tail therefore contains an alarm <almost surely>, even though the individual <probabilities> tend to zero.

\Image[borel-cantelli-series-and-tail-probabilities.png]
{title=Summable and harmonic probabilities: partial sums and the probability of a later event}
{description=The left panel shows finite <partial sums>. On the right, the blue curve is the infinite tail of the squared-reciprocal <p-series>, bounding the <probability> of a later <event> by the <union bound>; the gray curve is its $1/m$ upper bound. For harmonic <probabilities>, mutually <independent events> have a later occurrence with <probability> one, while the <shared-uniform counterexample to the second Borel-Cantelli lemma> has tail <probability> $1/(m+1)$. The infinite claims follow from the calculations, not from the finite plotted range.}

= Gaussian sample limsup at logarithmic scale
{c}
{parent=Borel-Cantelli lemmas}
{title2=$\limsup_{n\to\infty}X_n^2/\log n=2$}

For independent <standard normal random variables> $X_n$, the displayed <limit superior> equals two <almost surely>. The <Mills ratio> asymptotic gives, for $a>0$,
$$
\mathbb P(X_n^2>a\log n)\sim\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi a}}\frac{n^{-a/2}}{\sqrt{\log n}}.
$$
The series converges for $a>2$ and diverges for $0<a\leq2$. The two <Borel-Cantelli lemmas>, applied at countably many thresholds approaching two, give the result. In particular, the value two is also a threshold exceeded infinitely often, despite being the almost-sure <limit superior>.

= Borel-Cantelli first lemma
{c}
{parent=Borel-Cantelli lemmas}

= First Borel-Cantelli lemma
{synonym}
{c}

For arbitrary <events> $A_n$ in one <probability space>,
$$
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb P(A_n)<\infty
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\mathbb P\left(\limsup_{n\to\infty}A_n\right)=0.
$$
Equivalently, only finitely many <events> occur <almost surely>; no <independence> is needed.<Probability: Theory and Examples (fifth edition) by Rick Durrett>[§2.3, Theorem 2.3.1, printed p. 68]{ref}

Proof outline: put $B_m=\bigcup_{n\geq m}A_n$. The <union bound> gives
$$
0\leq\mathbb P\left(\limsup_n A_n\right)
\leq\mathbb P(B_m)\leq\sum_{n=m}^{\infty}\mathbb P(A_n)\longrightarrow0.
$$
The tail of a convergent nonnegative <series> tends to zero.

= Borel-Cantelli second lemma
{c}
{parent=Borel-Cantelli lemmas}

= Second Borel-Cantelli lemma
{c}
{synonym}

If the <events> $A_n$ are mutually <independent events> and $\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb P(A_n)=\infty$, then $\mathbb P(\limsup_n A_n)=1$. For an infinite family, mutual <independence> means every finite subfamily satisfies the factorization defining <independent events>.<Probability: Theory and Examples (fifth edition) by Rick Durrett>[§2.3, Theorem 2.3.7 and its proof, printed p. 70]{ref}

Proof outline: for $M\geq m$, <independent events> and $1-x\leq e^{-x}$ give
$$
\mathbb P\left(\bigcap_{n=m}^{M}A_n^c\right)
=\prod_{n=m}^{M}\left(1-\mathbb P(A_n)\right)
\leq\exp\left(-\sum_{n=m}^{M}\mathbb P(A_n)\right)\longrightarrow0.
$$
<Continuity of probability> makes each infinite tail <union> have <probability> one; their countable <intersection> has <probability> one as well.

= Shared-uniform counterexample to the second Borel-Cantelli lemma
{parent=Borel-Cantelli second lemma}

Choose one <random variable> $U$ with the <continuous uniform distribution> on $[0,1]$, and reuse it in every <event>:
$$
A_n=\left\{U\leq\frac1{n+1}\right\},\qquad n\geq1.
$$
Each <probability> is $1/(n+1)$, so their <harmonic series> diverges, just as in <summable and harmonic event probabilities>. But the <events> are decreasing: $A_{n+1}\subseteq A_n$. For every outcome $U=u>0$, all indices with $n+1>1/u$ fail. Only finitely many <events> occur on that outcome. The <limsup of events> is exactly $\{U=0\}$, a <zero-probability event>. This is a concrete closed-interval version of the shrinking-interval dependence example.<Probability: Theory and Examples (fifth edition) by Rick Durrett>[§2.3, Example 2.3.6, printed p. 70: shrinking intervals with divergent total probability]{ref}

The missing assumption is visible already in two <events>. For $i<j$, their <intersection> is $A_j$, hence
$$
\mathbb P(A_i\cap A_j)=\frac1{j+1}
\ne\frac1{(i+1)(j+1)}=\mathbb P(A_i)\mathbb P(A_j).
$$
Moreover, the whole tail <union> is just $A_m$, with <probability> $1/(m+1)\to0$. This calculation explains directly why divergence alone fails: overlapping <events> keep spending their individual <probabilities> on the same outcomes.

Let $N=\sum_{n\geq1}\mathbf1_{A_n}$ be the total number of occurrences, a sum of <indicator random variables>. Although $N<\infty$ <almost surely>, <Tonelli theorem> gives $\mathbb E[N]=\sum_{n\geq1}1/(n+1)=\infty$. More explicitly, $\mathbb P(N\geq k)=1/(k+1)$ for $k\geq1$: very small positive values of $U$ give very long, but finite, initial runs. Infinite <expected value> does not imply a positive <probability> of an infinite count.

\Image[borel-cantelli-independent-and-shared-uniform-traces.png]
{title=Equal harmonic event probabilities with fresh independent inputs or one shared input}
{description=Each row is one finite simulation of the <indicator random variables> of $A_n$ for $1\leq n\leq120$; a dot means the <event> occurred. Fresh <independent random variables> allow scattered returns; a shared <uniform random variable> yields an initial run followed by zeros. The horizontal axis uses a <logarithmic scale> to show early runs clearly. These finite simulations illustrate the two models and cannot prove infinitely many occurrences or eventual stopping.}

= Tail-sum formula for expectation
{parent=Borel-Cantelli lemmas}

For a nonnegative <random variable> $X$, <Tonelli theorem> gives
$$
\mathbb E[X]=\int_0^\infty\mathbb P(X>t)\,dt.
$$
Consequently, for each $a>0$, $\mathbb E[X]=\infty$ exactly when $\sum_{n\geq1}\mathbb P(X>an)=\infty$; compare the integral on successive intervals of length $a$.

= Extremes of independent exponential variables
{parent=Borel-Cantelli lemmas}

For independent rate-one exponential variables $X_n$ and $M_n=\max_{m\leq n}X_m$,
$$
\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{X_n}{\log n}=1,
\qquad
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{M_n}{\log n}\geq1
$$
almost surely. The first follows by applying both Borel--Cantelli lemmas to thresholds $(1\pm\varepsilon)\log n$; the second uses
$\mathbb P(M_n\leq(1-\varepsilon)\log n)\leq e^{-n^\varepsilon}$.

= Conditional Borel-Cantelli lemma
{c}
{parent=Borel-Cantelli lemmas}
{wiki}

For events $B_n\in\mathcal F_n$, put $p_n=\mathbb P(B_n\mid\mathcal F_{n-1})$. Then $\sum_n\mathbf1_{B_n}<\infty$ almost surely on $\{\sum_np_n<\infty\}$, while $\sum_n\mathbf1_{B_n}=\infty$ almost surely on $\{\sum_np_n=\infty\}$. The proof applies the <martingale convergence theorem> to compensated sums $\sum_{j\leq n}(\mathbf1_{B_j}-p_j)$ and uses <Kronecker lemma> in the divergent case.

\Include[stochastic-process]{parent=Probability theory}

= Lévy's continuity theorem
{parent=Probability theory}
{wiki}
{c}

= Characteristic-function convergence theorem
{synonym}

Pointwise convergence of characteristic functions to a function continuous at zero implies convergence in distribution.

= Poisson process
{c}
{parent=Probability theory}
{wiki}
{title2=$N_t$}

= Homogeneous Poisson process
{c}
{synonym}

A <homogeneous Poisson process> models arrivals at a constant rate $\lambda>0$. Its <counting process> $N_t=N(t)$ counts arrivals in $(0,t]$, with $N_0=0$. Its paths are <right-continuous> <nondecreasing functions>, take nonnegative <integer> values, and have unit jumps <almost surely>. The defining <Poisson distribution> of each increment is
$$
\mathbb P(N_{s+t}-N_s=k)=e^{-\lambda t}\frac{(\lambda t)^k}{k!},\qquad s\geq0,\quad t>0,\quad k=0,1,2,\ldots.
$$
It has <stationary increments>: the <probability distribution> above depends on the duration $t$, not the starting time $s$. It also has <independent increments>: counts in any finite collection of disjoint time intervals are <independent random variables>. Together these conditions specify the joint count laws, not merely the <Poisson distribution> at a single time.<Discrete Stochastic Processes (draft of 2nd edition, 31 January 2011) by Robert Gallager/Poisson Processes (Chapter 2)>[pp. 78-79: counting-process definition and its equivalence to exponential interarrival times]{ref}

The rate has units of arrivals per unit time; $\lambda t$ is dimensionless. The <Poisson distribution> gives
$$
\mathbb E[N_{s+t}-N_s]=\operatorname{Var}(N_{s+t}-N_s)=\lambda t.
$$
For a toy rate $\lambda=2$ per minute and a duration of $1.5$ minutes, the <expected value> and <variance> of the count are both $3$. The <probability> of no arrival is $e^{-3}\approx0.049787$, and the <probability> of exactly three is $3^3e^{-3}/3!=\tfrac92e^{-3}\approx0.224042$. In two consecutive one-minute intervals, the <probability> of exactly two arrivals in the first and exactly one in the second is $(2e^{-2})(2e^{-2})=4e^{-4}\approx0.073263$, using <independent increments>. These are exact model calculations with rounded decimals, not measured frequencies.

\Image[poisson-arrival-events-counting-path.png]{title=Synthetic arrival times and their right-continuous counting path, compared with the exact mean at two arrivals per minute}

The diagram's nine hand-chosen arrivals illustrate a path; the line $\lambda t$ is an <expected value>, not a prescribed trajectory. Constant rate does not imply evenly spaced arrivals. <Poisson interarrival times> connect the count description to waiting, while <Poisson thinning> explains how independently routing arrivals produces separate streams. This makes the <Poisson process> useful as a simple arrival model for a <queue>, provided constant rate and <independent increments> are plausible. For a deterministic changing rate, use an <inhomogeneous Poisson process>; bunching or history-dependent arrivals need additional assumptions. Conditional on $N_t=n$, the arrival times are the <order statistics> of $n$ <independent> <uniform distributions> on $[0,t]$, as developed in <Poisson process conditional arrival times>.

= Poisson interarrival times
{c}
{parent=Poisson process}
{title2=$W_n=T_n-T_{n-1}\sim\operatorname{Exp}(\lambda)$}

= Waiting times of a Poisson process
{c}
{synonym}

Let $T_n=\inf\{t\geq0:N_t\geq n\}$ be the $n$th arrival time, and set $T_0=0$. In a <homogeneous Poisson process> of rate $\lambda>0$, the gaps $W_n=T_n-T_{n-1}$ are <independent and identically distributed random variables> with an <exponential distribution>:
$$
\mathbb P(W_n>w)=e^{-\lambda w},\qquad f_{W_n}(w)=\lambda e^{-\lambda w},\qquad w\geq0;
\qquad \mathbb E[W_n]=\frac1\lambda,\quad \operatorname{Var}(W_n)=\frac1{\lambda^2}.
$$
Here $\operatorname{Exp}(\lambda)$ uses the rate convention. Under the scale convention $\theta=1/\lambda$, the same <probability density function> is $\theta^{-1}e^{-w/\theta}$; a simulation function accepting a scale must receive $1/\lambda$. Conversely, summing <independent> rate-$\lambda$ <exponential random variables> to form $T_n$ and counting those sums constructs a <homogeneous Poisson process>.<Lectures on the Poisson Process by Günter Last and others>[Theorem 7.2, printed p. 59: interval theorem characterizing independent exponential gaps]{ref}

The first-gap formula follows immediately from $\{W_1>w\}=\{N_w=0\}$ and the <Poisson distribution>. Independence of all successive gaps is the additional interval-theorem assertion; deterministic-time <independent increments> alone should not simply be substituted at random arrival times without justification. The <memorylessness of the exponential distribution> gives
$$
\mathbb P(W_n>a+w\mid W_n>a)=e^{-\lambda w},\qquad a,w\geq0.
$$
At the toy rate of two per minute, the <mean> gap is half a minute, the <median> is $(\log2)/2\approx0.346574$ minutes, and the <probability> of waiting longer than half a minute is $e^{-1}\approx0.367879$. Here $\log$ is the <natural logarithm>. Already having waited a minute without an arrival leaves this same <conditional probability> of waiting another half minute. The <exponential distribution> describes a model with no aging of the remaining wait; a scheduled arrival with a fixed gap has no such property.

= Poisson observation scheme and rate likelihood
{parent=Poisson process}
{title2=$\widehat\lambda=N_H/H\quad\text{or}\quad n/T_n$}

A <Poisson process> observed for a fixed duration $H$ has likelihood proportional to $\lambda^{N_H}e^{-\lambda H}$. Complete independent <exponential distribution> gaps observed until a fixed event count $n$ have likelihood $\lambda^ne^{-\lambda T_n}$. Both give count divided by exposure, but the exposure is random in the second design. Under fixed exposure the total count is sufficient: conditional on it, the event locations are independent of the rate, so exact timestamps add no rate information to interval counts. Gap estimates and fixed-horizon estimates should not be compared without distinguishing their stopping rules and any final censored gap.

= Strong law for Poisson arrival times
{parent=Poisson process}
{title2=$Y_n/n\to1/\lambda$}

For a homogeneous <Poisson process> of rate $\lambda>0$, $N(t)/t\to\lambda$ almost surely: unit-time increments are independent Poisson variables and the <strong law of large numbers> applies, then monotonicity fills the intervals between integers. At its $n$th point $Y_n$, simplicity gives $N(Y_n)=n$. Consequently $Y_n/n\to1/\lambda$. Rate and mean interarrival time are reciprocals.

= Poisson exponential martingale
{parent=Poisson process}
{c}
{title2=$M_t^\theta=\exp(\theta N_t-t(e^\theta-1))$}

For a unit-rate <Poisson process> and real $\theta$, the displayed process is a nonnegative <martingale> with initial value one. Independent increments give $\mathbb E e^{\theta(N_t-N_s)}=\exp((t-s)(e^\theta-1))$, proving its conditional mean property. The <Ville inequality> turns this exponential moment into a maximal tail bound.

= Poisson maximal concentration bound
{parent=Poisson process}
{c}
{title2=$\Pr(\sup_{t\leq L}|N(t)-t|\geq L\eta)\leq2e^{-Lh(\eta)}$}

For a unit-rate <Poisson process>, $h(\eta)=(1+\eta)\log(1+\eta)-\eta$ gives the displayed maximal deviation bound for $L,\eta>0$. <Poisson exponential martingales> and the <Ville inequality> control upper and lower deviations; the upper-tail rate $h$ also bounds the lower-tail probability. Since $h(0)=0$ and $h'(\eta)=\log(1+\eta)>0$, the rate is strictly positive for every positive deviation level.

= Every second Poisson arrival has underdispersed counts
{c}
{parent=Poisson process}
{title2=$M(t)=\lfloor N(t)/2\rfloor$}

For a rate-$\lambda$ <Poisson process>, keeping every second arrival gives a renewal process with independent interarrival times equal to sums of two exponential waiting times. Put $x=\lambda t$. Its count mean and variance are $(2x+e^{-2x}-1)/4$ and $(4x-8xe^{-2x}-e^{-4x}+1)/16$. The variance is strictly smaller than the mean for $x>0$: sixteen times their difference has derivative $4[-1+4xe^{-2x}+e^{-4x}]<0$ and is zero at zero, because $\sinh(2x)>2x$. Therefore this process is not a <Cox process>.

= Cox process
{c}
{parent=Poisson process}
{title2=$N\mid\Lambda\sim\operatorname{Poisson\ process}(\Lambda)$}
{wiki}

= Doubly-stochastic Poisson process
{synonym}

A counting process which, conditional on a random nonnegative locally integrable intensity $\Lambda$, is an <inhomogeneous Poisson process>. For $L_t=\int_0^t\Lambda(s)\,ds$, conditioning gives $\mathbb EN(t)=\mathbb EL_t$ and $\operatorname{Var}N(t)=\mathbb EL_t+\operatorname{Var}L_t$. Thus finite-variance counts are never underdispersed. Randomness of the intensity may induce dependence between increments which are independent conditionally.

= Poisson offspring clusters directed by their parent process
{parent=Cox process}
{c}
{title2=$\Lambda_\Pi(A)=\mu\sum_{z\in\Pi}\kappa(A-z)$}

When each parent has a Poisson number of independently displaced offspring, the conditional offspring process has the displayed directing measure. For disjoint target sets, <multinomial distribution> splitting of a parent's Poisson count gives joint <probability generating function> $\exp(\mu\sum_j\kappa(A_j-z)(s_j-1))$. Multiply over <independent> parents to obtain <independent> Poisson counts conditional on the parents. Compactly supported displacement ensures local finiteness for a locally finite parent process.

// Destination: discrete-probability-distribution.bigb

= Random directing measure of a Cox process
{parent=Cox process}
{title2=$N\mid\Lambda\sim\operatorname{PRM}(\Lambda)$}

A <Cox process> can be directed by a random locally <finite measure>, which need not have a density. Conditional counts in disjoint sets are <independent> Poisson variables with that measure's masses. Uniform circular offspring displacements give a directing measure supported on random circles; its unconditional mean can still be a multiple of planar <Lebesgue measure>. A random intensity function is the special case in which the directing measure is absolutely continuous.

= Inhomogeneous Poisson process
{c}
{parent=Poisson process}
{wiki}

An inhomogeneous Poisson process with locally integrable intensity $\lambda(t)\geq0$ has independent increments and
$$
N_t-N_s\sim\operatorname{Poisson}\left(\int_s^t\lambda(u)\,du\right)
$$
for $0\leq s<t$. Its cumulative intensity is $\Lambda(t)=\int_0^t\lambda(u)\,du$.

= Time change of an inhomogeneous Poisson process
{parent=Inhomogeneous Poisson process}

If $\lambda>0$ and $g=\Lambda^{-1}$, then $M_t=N_{g(t)}$ is a rate-one <Poisson process>, because
$$
\Lambda(g(t))-\Lambda(g(s))=t-s.
$$
Conversely, $N_t=M_{\Lambda(t)}$, so $N_t$ has a Poisson distribution of mean $\Lambda(t)$.

= Symmetric Poisson difference process
{parent=Poisson process}

The difference of two independent rate-$\lambda$ <Poisson processes> is a continuous-time simple symmetric random walk with total jump rate $2\lambda$ and variance $2\lambda t$ at time $t$.

= Poisson thinning
{c}
{parent=Poisson process}

<Poisson thinning> splits a <Poisson process> by independently marking each arrival as retained or rejected. Let the original <homogeneous Poisson process> have rate $\lambda>0$, and let $p(s)\in[0,1]$ be a deterministic <measurable function> of time. Attach <independent> <uniform distributions> $U_1,U_2,\ldots$ on $(0,1)$, independent of the entire original <Poisson process>. At arrival time $T_i$, retain the arrival if $U_i\leq p(T_i)$. Given all arrival times, these decisions are <independent> and have the specified retention <probabilities>. Define the two <counting processes> using an <indicator function>:
$$
R_t=\sum_{i:T_i\leq t}\mathbf1_{\{U_i\leq p(T_i)\}},\qquad D_t=N_t-R_t.
$$
The <Poisson thinning theorem> gives <independent> retained and rejected <inhomogeneous Poisson processes>. Their rates are $\lambda p(s)$ and $\lambda(1-p(s))$, respectively, in the sense of the <intensity function of a point process>. In particular, for $0\leq a<b$,
$$
R_b-R_a\sim\operatorname{Poisson}\!\left(\lambda\int_a^b p(s)\,ds\right),\qquad D_b-D_a\sim\operatorname{Poisson}\!\left(\lambda\int_a^b(1-p(s))\,ds\right).
$$
The <independence> is of the two whole processes: every finite collection of retained counts is <independent> of every finite collection of rejected counts. It is not merely zero <covariance> for one interval. For constant $p$, the streams are <homogeneous Poisson processes> of rates $\lambda p$ and $\lambda(1-p)$; the endpoints $p=0,1$ include the identically zero stream.<Lectures on the Poisson Process by Günter Last and others>[Definition 5.7, Proposition 5.5, Theorem 5.8 and Corollary 5.9, printed pp. 42-44: location-dependent independent marking, intensities, and independence of the split processes]{ref}

For an exact toy calculation, take $\lambda=2$ per minute and retain with $p(s)=1/4$ on $(0,2]$ and $p(s)=3/4$ on $(2,4]$. In the first two minutes, the retained and rejected <expected values> are $2\cdot2\cdot1/4=1$ and $2\cdot2\cdot3/4=3$. In the next two minutes they are $3$ and $1$. Over all four minutes, $R_4,D_4$ are <independent> <Poisson distributions> with <mean> $4$ each, and their sum $N_4$ has <mean> $8$. Thus
$$
\mathbb P(R_4=0,D_4=0)=e^{-4}e^{-4}=e^{-8},\qquad \mathbb P(R_4=2,D_4=3)=e^{-8}\frac{4^2}{2!}\frac{4^3}{3!}=\frac{256}{3}e^{-8}.
$$
These <probabilities> and <expected values> describe the model, not empirical measurements. In the diagram, the original nine synthetic arrivals split into five retained and four rejected arrivals; those realized counts need not equal their <expected values>.

\Image[poisson-independent-thinning-expected-counts.png]{title=Independent marks split a synthetic Poisson arrival path, with exact expected counts for two time-dependent retention probabilities}

The mechanism explains why mutually exclusive labels can yield <independent> counts: randomness of the original total offsets the dependence from allocating a fixed total. For constant $p$ and an interval with original <mean> $m$, the conditional retained count has a <binomial distribution> given $N=n$. The joint <probability generating function> is
$$
\mathbb E[z^Rw^D]=\mathbb E[(pz+(1-p)w)^N]=\exp\{m(pz+(1-p)w-1)\}=e^{mp(z-1)}e^{m(1-p)(w-1)},\qquad 0\leq z,w\leq1.
$$
This factorization proves the two <independent> <Poisson distributions>; disjoint original intervals give <independent> pairs. For varying $p(s)$, the <independent marking theorem for Poisson point processes> gives the same conclusion with the two integrated intensities. The <joint red and green Poisson count formula> gives the corresponding direct calculation with <probability mass functions>.

Independence here is unconditional. After conditioning on $N=n$, the identity $D=n-R$ gives <covariance> $\operatorname{Cov}(R,D\mid N=n)=-np(1-p)$ for constant $p$. Thus the retained and rejected counts are dependent for $n>0$ and $0<p<1$, even though their unconditional <covariance> is zero. <Poisson thinning> can model independent routing of arrivals, or independent detection of arrivals with a known efficiency schedule; using only a retention fraction without checking the marking mechanism can give a wrong count law.

= Dependence in Poisson thinning
{c}
{parent=Poisson thinning}

Independent marking is the hypothesis that guarantees the <Poisson thinning theorem>. Equal marginal retention <probabilities> do not guarantee it. Consider one <Bernoulli random variable> $B$ with success <probability> $p\in(0,1)$, independent of a <Poisson process>, and retain every arrival when $B=1$ and none when $B=0$. Each arrival is retained with <probability> $p$, but the marks share the same coin. In an interval whose original count has <Poisson distribution> of <mean> $m>0$, the retained count is $R=BN$ and the rejected count is $D=(1-B)N$, so
$$
\mathbb E[R]=pm,\qquad \operatorname{Var}(R)=pm+p(1-p)m^2,\qquad \operatorname{Cov}(R,D)=-p(1-p)m^2.
$$
The extra <variance> shows that $R$ is not <Poisson distributed>; the negative <covariance> proves dependence. Its no-arrival <probability> is $1-p+pe^{-m}$, rather than $e^{-pm}$. For the toy values $p=1/2,m=2$, both rules have retained <mean> $1$, but the common-coin rule has <variance> $2$ and no-arrival <probability> $(1+e^{-2})/2\approx0.567668$, compared with <variance> $1$ and no-arrival <probability> $e^{-1}\approx0.367879$ for independent marking. These formulas follow by conditioning on $B$ and using the <law of total variance>.

An arrival-dependent or adversarial retention rule can change the law just as strongly. Keeping every second arrival gives $R_t=\lfloor N_t/2\rfloor$. Its successive retained gaps are sums of two <independent> <exponential random variables>, hence have the <Erlang distribution> of shape two and rate $\lambda$, not an <exponential distribution>. The resulting <renewal process> and its count <variance> are analyzed in <every second Poisson arrival has underdispersed counts>, and the two dependent streams in <alternating split of a Poisson process>. As an even more restrictive rule, keeping only the first arrival ever gives $R_t=\mathbf1_{\{N_t\geq1\}}$, a <Bernoulli distribution> at each $t>0$, so it cannot have a positive-mean <Poisson distribution>. A thinning rule must specify how decisions depend on arrivals and on one another; the independent-marking conclusion cannot be applied automatically to arbitrary deletion.

= Poisson characterization by independent binomial splitting
{c}
{parent=Poisson thinning}

Let $N$ be a nonnegative integer-valued <random variable> with finite <expected value> $\mu$, and independently label each of its items with either of two equally likely labels. If the two resulting counts are <independent random variables>, then $N$ has a <Poisson distribution>. Indeed, its <probability generating function> obeys $G(s)=G((1+s)/2)^2$. With $H(t)=G(1-t)$, iteration gives $H(t)=H(t/2^k)^{2^k}$. Finite <expected value> gives $H(h)=1-\mu h+o(h)$, hence $H(t)=e^{-\mu t}$ and $G(s)=e^{\mu(s-1)}$. Conversely, <Poisson thinning> produces independent <Poisson distributions> for the two counts.

= Categorical thinning of a Poisson claim count
{parent=Poisson thinning}
{title2=$N_i\text{ independent},\quad N_i\sim\operatorname{Pois}(\lambda p_i)$}

Assign each of $N\sim\operatorname{Pois}(\lambda)$ claims with <independent> category labels to one of finitely many categories with <probabilities> $p_i$. Conditional counts have a <multinomial distribution> with <probability generating function> $(\sum_i p_i z_i)^N$. Averaging over $N$ gives $\exp(\lambda(\sum_i p_i z_i-1))=\prod_i\exp(\lambda p_i(z_i-1))$. This proves <independent> category counts and supports discrete-severity <compound Poisson distribution> representations.

= Poisson onset-bin independence
{parent=Poisson thinning}
{c}

Mark each member of an independent Poisson infection cohort by its symptom-onset interval, using independent delays. <Poisson thinning> gives independent onset-bin counts, and sums over independent cohorts remain independent Poisson counts. Fixed cohort totals instead induce multinomial dependence. The marking assumptions are essential.

= Binomial thinning conditional on a total
{parent=Poisson thinning}

Conditioned on a total of $n$ Poisson points, the number falling in a region of relative intensity $p$ is $\operatorname{Binomial}(n,p)$.

= Infinite-server queue transient distribution
{parent=Poisson thinning}

For an <M-M-infinity queue> with empty initial state, Poisson arrivals of rate $\lambda$ and exponential lifetimes of rate $\mu$ produce a Poisson occupancy of mean $\lambda(1-e^{-\mu t})/\mu$ at time $t$.
