For $d$ independent centered <normal random variables> of common <variance> $v$, $|\mathbf X|^2/v$ has a <chi-squared distribution> with $d$ degrees of freedom. Its density gives the displayed moments by a <gamma integral>. In three dimensions, $\mathbb E|\mathbf X|^{2n}=(2n+1)!!v^n$. Radial odd moments need not vanish, although any odd Cartesian product does.

= Chi-bar-squared distribution
{parent=Chi-squared distribution}
{title2=$\sum_jw_j\chi_j^2$}

A chi-bar-squared distribution is a <mixture distribution> $\sum_{j=0}^m w_j\chi_j^2$, where $w_j\geq0$ and $\sum_jw_j=1$, and $\chi_0^2$ denotes a point mass at zero. It arises when a Gaussian <likelihood> is optimized under inequality constraints: projection onto a constraint cone selects different dimensions according to which inequalities are active. For example, if $W,Z$ are independent standard <Gaussian random variables>, then $Z^2+(-W)_+^2$ has distribution $\tfrac12\chi_1^2+\tfrac12\chi_2^2$. On $W\geq0$ it is $Z^2$; on $W<0$ it is $Z^2+W^2$. Symmetry makes the sign of $W$ independent of $W^2$, proving the stated weights and component laws.

= Noncentral chi-squared distribution
{parent=Chi-squared distribution}
{title2=$\chi^2_\nu(\lambda)$}

If $Z_1,\ldots,Z_\nu$ are independent <standard normal random variables> and $\lambda=\sum_j\mu_j^2$, then $\sum_j(Z_j+\mu_j)^2$ has a noncentral chi-squared distribution with $\nu$ degrees of freedom and noncentrality $\lambda$. For $\lambda=0$ it reduces to the <chi-squared distribution>. Squaring a shifted <signed normal Wald statistic> gives the case $\nu=1$.

= Reciprocal moment of a noncentral chi-squared variable
{parent=Noncentral chi-squared distribution}
{title2=$\mathbb E\frac1Q=\int_0^\infty(1+2t)^{-m/2}e^{-\lambda t/(1+2t)}dt$}

For $Q\sim\chi_m^2(\lambda)$ with $m>2$, completing the square in its Gaussian representation gives $\mathbb Ee^{-tQ}=(1+2t)^{-m/2}e^{-\lambda t/(1+2t)}$. Integrate the identity $1/q=\int_0^\infty e^{-tq}dt$ and use <Tonelli theorem> to obtain the displayed reciprocal moment. It is strictly decreasing in $\lambda\geq0$, since its positive integrand is strictly decreasing for every $t>0$. At $\lambda=0$ the integral equals $1/(m-2)$, proving finiteness and the sharp maximum over noncentrality parameters.

= Chi-squared concentration inequality
{parent=Chi-squared distribution}
{title2=$\mathbb P(V-n\geq2\sqrt{nx}+2x)\leq e^{-x}$}

For $V\sim\chi_n^2$ and $x>0$, the upper tail is at most $e^{-x}$ above $n+2\sqrt{nx}+2x$, and the lower tail is at most $e^{-x}$ below $n-2\sqrt{nx}$. Indeed $\log\mathbb E e^{s(V-n)}=-ns-(n/2)\log(1-2s)\leq ns^2/(1-2s)$ for $0<s<1/2$. The <Chernoff bound> with $s=\sqrt{x/n}/(1+2\sqrt{x/n})$ proves the upper estimate. For the lower estimate, $\log\mathbb E e^{-s(V-n)}\leq ns^2$ for $s>0$, and optimization gives the result. The lower threshold can be negative; the <chi-squared Chernoff lower-tail bound> provides an always-positive alternative.

= Chi-squared Chernoff lower-tail bound
{parent=Chi-squared concentration inequality}
{title2=$\mathbb P(V\leq na)\leq e^{-n(a-1-\log a)/2}$}

For $V\sim\chi_n^2$ and $0<a<1$, apply the <Chernoff bound> to $e^{-sV}$ using $\mathbb E e^{-sV}=(1+2s)^{-n/2}$. Optimization at $s=(a^{-1}-1)/2$ proves the displayed estimate. It remains useful when the additive lower threshold in the <chi-squared concentration inequality> is nonpositive. For $0<r\leq1$, choosing $a=e^{-4r}$ gives a tail at most $e^{-nr^2}$ because $e^{-4r}-1+4r\geq2r^2$.

= Moment-generating function of a chi-squared distribution
{parent=Chi-squared distribution}
{title2=$M_{\chi_n^2}(t)=(1-2t)^{-n/2}$}

A sum of the squares of $n$ <independent random variables> with the <standard normal distribution> has the <chi-squared distribution> with $n$ degrees of freedom. A scaled <Gaussian integral> and factorization by independence give its <moment-generating function> $(1-2t)^{-n/2}$ for $t<1/2$. For positive $n$, this <expectation> diverges when $t\geq1/2$.

= Scaled inverse chi-squared distribution
{title2=$\operatorname{Inv}\text{-}\chi^2(\nu,b^2)$}
{parent=Chi-squared distribution}
{wiki}

The scaled inverse chi-squared distribution has density
$$
f(x)\propto x^{-(\nu/2+1)}\exp\!\left(-\frac{\nu b^2}{2x}\right),
\qquad x>0.
$$
It is an <inverse-gamma distribution> under a change of parameters.

= Chi-squared stochastic ordering
{parent=Chi-squared distribution}

If $r<s$, coupling by extra independent squared normals gives $\chi_s^2=\chi_r^2+W$ with $W\geq0$, so every fixed upper quantile increases with the degrees of freedom.

= Chi-squared asymptotic approximation
{parent=Chi-squared distribution}

Many quadratic and likelihood-ratio statistics converge under a regular null hypothesis to a <chi-squared distribution>. Using its quantiles for a finite sample is accurate when the sample is large relative to the model dimension and expected category counts are not too small.

= Weibull distribution
{parent=Probability distribution}
{c}
{wiki}

The Weibull distribution has density $k\lambda^{-1}(y/\lambda)^{k-1}e^{-(y/\lambda)^k}$ on $y>0$.

= Unknown entry age in a Weibull survival segment
{parent=Weibull distribution}

Conditional survival for a segment of length $u$ entered at age $\tau$ is $\exp\{-\lambda^p[(\tau+u)^p-\tau^p]\}$ under inverse-scale parameter $\lambda$. It depends on the entry age unless $p=1$. Unknown ages require a specified entry-age model or recovered history; merely adding equally unspecified segments does not supply the missing likelihood information.

= Characteristic function of a uniform distribution
{parent=Probability distribution}

For $X$ uniform on $[-a,a]$, $\phi_X(t)=\sin(at)/(at)$.

= Dyadic cosine product
{parent=Characteristic function of a uniform distribution}
{title2=$\sin t/t=\prod_{j\geq1}\cos(t/2^j)$}

The <characteristic function> of a <dyadic Rademacher series> is $\sin t/t$, interpreted as one at zero. The <characteristic function of a sum of independent variables> gives the finite cosine products, and <weak convergence of probability measures> gives their displayed limit for every real $t$. This argument includes zeros of the <sinc function> without taking logarithms of the factors.

= Small-argument sine product asymptotic
{parent=Characteristic function of a uniform distribution}

The expansion $\log(\sin x/x)=-x^2/6+O(x^4)$ converts products of small sinc factors into Gaussian characteristic-function limits.

= Variance of a uniform distribution
{parent=Probability distribution}

A uniform variable on $[-a,a]$ has mean zero and variance $a^2/3$.

= Exponential survival probability
{parent=Probability distribution}

A lifetime exponential with rate $\mu$ survives for at least duration $u$ with probability $e^{-\mu u}$.

= Expected value
{title2=$\mathbb E[X]$}
{parent=Probability theory}
{wiki}

= Expectation
{synonym}

= Mean
{synonym}

= Ensemble average
{synonym}

An <expected value> is the <probability>-weighted <arithmetic mean> of a <real-valued random variable>. On a <probability space> $(\Omega,\mathcal F,\mathbb P)$, its general definition is the <Lebesgue integral>
$$
\mathbb E[X]=\int_\Omega X\,d\mathbb P=\int_{\mathbb R}x\,d\mu_X(x),
$$
where $\mu_X$ is the <probability distribution> of $X$. For an <integrable random variable>, this is a finite <real number>. With a <probability mass function> $p_X$, use $\sum_x xp_X(x)$; with a <probability density function> $f_X$, use $\int_{\mathbb R}xf_X(x)\,dx$. These are two ways of computing the same weighted average, rather than different definitions for different experiments.

For example, suppose $X$ takes the values $-1,2,8$ with <probabilities> $1/2,3/10,1/5$. Then
$$
\boxed{\mathbb E[X]=(-1)\frac12+2\frac3{10}+8\frac15=\frac{17}{10}=1.7.}
$$
The most likely outcome is $-1$, and $1.7$ is not a possible outcome at all. The <expected value> summarizes all outcomes and their weights; it need not predict the result of one trial. Geometrically, placing <masses> equal to the <probability masses> at the outcome positions gives a <center of mass> at $1.7$, since $\sum_xp_X(x)(x-1.7)=0$. This balance-point interpretation also explains the connection to a <weighted arithmetic mean>. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-05-introduction-to-probability-and-statistics-spring-2022/mit18_05_s22_class04-prep-b.pdf[Orloff and Bloom, expected value: probability weights and center of mass]{ref}.

For the dimensionless <random variable> $Y$ with <probability density function> $f_Y(y)=2y$ on $[0,1]$, the <expected value> is $\mathbb E[Y]=\int_0^1y(2y)\,dy=2/3$. Integrating the <probability density function> itself gives one; to compute the <expected value>, multiply by the outcome first. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-05-introduction-to-probability-and-statistics-spring-2022/mit18_05_s22_class06-prep-a.pdf[Orloff and Bloom, expectation for continuous random variables]{ref}.

\Image[expected-value-balance.png]
{title=<Expected value> balances <probability masses>, or integrates outcomes weighted by a <probability density function>}
{description=The left panel's dashed line marks $1.7$, even though the only possible outcomes are $-1,2,8$. In the right panel, the shaded <area> under $y f_Y(y)=2y^2$ is $2/3$; the <area> under the dashed <probability density function> is one. Both examples use dimensionless outcomes.}

Why is this useful? <Linearity of expectation> lets us compute an average total by adding average contributions, even for <dependent random variables>. And for <independent and identically distributed> <integrable random variables>, the <strong law of large numbers> says their <sample mean> approaches their common <expected value> <almost surely>. The theorem supplies the link to repeated observations; the weighted-average definition itself does not promise that any particular finite run will be close to the <expected value>.

There is a useful historical connection to valuing uncertain rewards. In his 1657 *De ratiociniis in ludo aleae*, <Christiaan Huygens> assigns the value $(a+b)/2$ to equally likely rewards $a,b$, and $(pa+qb)/(p+q)$ when they have $p,q$ equally likely chances. These are the finite weighted-average formulas for <expected value>. His opening discussion asks how to divide the stakes when a game stops before completion, connecting <probability> to a concrete question about an unfinished contest. https://math.dartmouth.edu/~doyle/docs/huygens/huygens.pdf[Huygens, De ratiociniis in ludo aleae, 1657; reprint of the 1714 English translation, introduction and Propositions I–III]{ref}.

= Law of the unconscious statistician
{parent=Expected value}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathbb E[g(X)]=\int g(x)\,d\mu_X(x)$}

= Expected value rule
{synonym}

The <law of the unconscious statistician> computes the <expected value> of a <measurable function> of a <random variable> directly from the original <probability distribution>. If $g(X)$ is an <integrable random variable>, then
$$
\mathbb E[g(X)]=\int_{\mathbb R}g(x)\,d\mu_X(x).
$$
Use $\sum_xg(x)p_X(x)$ for a <probability mass function>, or $\int_{\mathbb R}g(x)f_X(x)\,dx$ for a <probability density function>. There is no need to derive the <probability distribution> of $g(X)$ first. The identity is the integration rule for a <pushforward measure>, and also holds with an infinite value when $g$ is nonnegative. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/res-6-012-introduction-to-probability-spring-2018/pages/part-i-the-fundamentals/[Tsitsiklis, Introduction to Probability, Lecture 5: the expected value rule]{ref}.

For $X$ taking $-1,2,8$ with <probabilities> $1/2,3/10,1/5$, choosing $g(x)=x^2$ gives $\mathbb E[X^2]=\tfrac12+4\tfrac3{10}+64\tfrac15=14.5$. Its <variance> is therefore $14.5-1.7^2=11.61$. Squaring the <expected value> would instead give $2.89$, because squaring does not commute with averaging.

= Outer expectation
{parent=Expected value}
{title2=$\mathbb E^*Z=\inf\{\mathbb EY:Y\geq Z,\ Y\text{ measurable}\}$}

For a bounded possibly nonmeasurable real function $Z$ on a <probability space>, its outer expectation is the infimum of <expected values> of measurable majorants. Its inner expectation is $\mathbb E_*Z=-\mathbb E^*(-Z)$. They agree with the usual <expected value> for measurable $Z$. This permits function-space <convergence in distribution> for an <empirical distribution process> whose paths are not Borel-measurable in a nonseparable norm topology: both expectations of each bounded continuous test functional must approach its ordinary expectation under the tight measurable limiting law.

= Expectation of a product of independent random variables
{parent=Expected value}
{title2=$\mathbb E\prod_iX_i=\prod_i\mathbb E X_i$}

For independent integrable variables, $\mathbb E\prod_iX_i=\prod_i\mathbb E X_i$. <Tonelli theorem> first gives $\mathbb E\prod_i|X_i|=\prod_i\mathbb E|X_i|<\infty$; then <Fubini's theorem> factors the signed joint integral.

= Mean-zero function
{parent=Expected value}
{title2=$\mathbb E f=0$}

A mean-zero function has <expectation> zero with respect to the specified <probability measure>. On a <finite set> with uniform measure this means $\sum_xf(x)=0$. A <balanced subset indicator> is an example. In <Fourier analysis on a finite group>, this is equivalent to vanishing of the component at the <trivial representation>.

= Integrable random variable
{parent=Expected value}
{title2=$\mathbb E|X|<\infty$}

A real or complex <random variable> $X$ is integrable when $\mathbb E|X|<\infty$. This is <integrability> with respect to its <probability measure>, and ensures that its <expected value> is finite. A bounded <random variable> is always an <integrable random variable>; so is the example taking $-1,2,8$, for which $\mathbb E|X|=2.7$.

For a real <random variable>, put $X_+=\max(X,0)$ and $X_-=\max(-X,0)$, its <positive part> and <negative part>. Each has a nonnegative <Lebesgue integral>, possibly infinite. The expression $\mathbb E[X]=\mathbb E[X_+]-\mathbb E[X_-]$ is defined when at least one of these two <expected values> is finite; if both are infinite, the <expected value> is undefined. <Integrability> makes both finite. A nonnegative <random variable> can have <expected value> $+\infty$, even though it is not an <integrable random variable>.

The standard <Cauchy distribution> is a useful warning. With its <probability density function> $f(x)=1/[\pi(1+x^2)]$, the positive contribution is $\int_0^Rxf(x)\,dx=\log(1+R^2)/(2\pi)$, which tends to infinity; the negative contribution has the same magnitude. Integrating symmetrically over $[-R,R]$ gives zero for each $R$, but this cancellation does not define an <expected value>. The <Cauchy sample mean has an unchanged distribution>, showing why the <integrability> hypothesis in the <strong law of large numbers> matters.

For an <integrable random variable>, <conditional expectation> preserves <integrability>, and <linearity of expectation> can safely combine finite averages without forming $\infty-\infty$.

= Moment
{title2=$\mathbb E[X^n]$}
{parent=Expected value}
{wiki=Moment_(mathematics)}

The $n$th raw moment of a random variable $X$ is $\mathbb E[X^n]$ when this expectation exists.

More generally, a <moment> is an integral weighted by a power of a coordinate against a measure or density; raw, central, and standardized moments are distinguished by the coordinate and normalization used.

= Fourth moment
{parent=Moment}
{title2=$\mathbb E[X^4]$}

The <fourth moment> is $\mathbb E[X^4]$. Its finiteness is equivalent to square integrability of $X^2$. For centered variables, dividing it by the squared <variance> gives <kurtosis>.

= Moment determinacy
{parent=Moment}

= Moment-determinate probability distribution
{synonym}

A <probability distribution> is moment-determinate if its moments uniquely specify it among <probability distributions>. A <moment-generating function> finite in a neighbourhood of zero is a sufficient condition. The <standard normal distribution> is moment-determinate.

= Second moment
{parent=Moment}
{title2=$\mathbb E[X^2]$}

The second <moment> of a real <random variable> is $\mathbb E[X^2]$. Its <variance> is $\mathbb E[X^2]-(\mathbb EX)^2$ when these quantities are finite.

= Absolute moment
{title2=$\mathbb E[|X|^p]$}
{parent=Moment}

The absolute moment of order $p>0$ of a <random variable> $X$ is $\mathbb E[|X|^p]$ when this expectation is finite.

= Central moment
{title2=$\mathbb E[(X-\mathbb EX)^n]$}
{parent=Moment}
{wiki}

The $n$th central moment is $\mathbb E[(X-\mathbb EX)^n]$. The first central moment is zero and the second is the <variance>.

= Skewness
{title2=$\gamma_1=\mathbb E[(X-\mathbb EX)^3]/\sigma^3$}
{parent=Central moment}
{wiki}

Skewness is the standardized third central <moment> of a <probability distribution>. A symmetric distribution with finite third <moment> has zero skewness. Cosmology also uses the reduced skewness $S_3=\langle\delta^3\rangle/\sigma^4$, which differs from the standardized skewness by a factor of $\sigma$.

\Include[variance]{parent=Expected value}

= Indicator random variable
{parent=Expected value}
{title2=$\mathbf1_A$}

An <indicator random variable> is the <indicator function> of an <event> $A$ in a <probability space>: $\mathbf1_A$ is one when $A$ occurs and zero otherwise. It has a <Bernoulli distribution> with parameter $p=\mathbb P(A)$, so $\mathbb E[\mathbf1_A]=p$ and $\operatorname{Var}(\mathbf1_A)=p(1-p)$. Even an <event> described without numbers becomes a numerical <random variable> in this way.

For a finite collection of <events>, their <indicator random variables> add up to the number that occur. <Linearity of expectation> therefore turns an average count into a sum of <probabilities>, without requiring <independence>. The <fixed point count of a uniform random permutation> provides an example where the indicators are <dependent random variables>.

= Tail-sum formula
{parent=Expected value}

For nonnegative $Y$, $E[Y]=\int_0^\infty P(Y\ge t)\,dt$; for nonnegative integer $N$, $E[N]=\sum_{k\ge1}P(N\ge k)$.

= Integer-valued hypothesis in the tail-sum formula
{parent=Tail-sum formula}
{title2=$\sum_{m\ge0}\mathbb P(X>m)=\mathbb E\lceil X\rceil\quad(X\geq0)$}

For a nonnegative integer-valued variable, $X=\sum_{m\ge0}\mathbf1_{\{X>m\}}$ and the <tail-sum formula> gives $\mathbb E X=\sum_{m\ge0}\mathbb P(X>m)$. For arbitrary positive real values, that integer-indexed sum equals $\mathbb E\lceil X\rceil$ instead. Two independent constants equal to $3/2$ demonstrate that the product of such tail sums need not equal the tail sum of their product: the two sides are four and three.

= Linearity of expectation
{parent=Expected value}
{title2=$\mathbb E[aX+bY]=a\mathbb E[X]+b\mathbb E[Y]$}

For <integrable random variables> $X_1,\ldots,X_n$ on the same <probability space> and fixed <real numbers> $a_1,\ldots,a_n$, <linearity of expectation> states
$$
\mathbb E\!\left[\sum_{i=1}^n a_iX_i\right]=\sum_{i=1}^n a_i\mathbb E[X_i].
$$
The <random variables> can be <dependent random variables>. The formula follows by the <linearity of integration>; a finite <linear combination> of <integrable random variables> remains <integrable>. For example, if $X$ takes $-1,2,8$ with <probabilities> $1/2,3/10,1/5$, then $X$ and $2X$ are <dependent random variables>, yet $\mathbb E[X+2X]=3\mathbb E[X]=5.1$.

An especially useful application counts how many <events> occur. Put $I_i=\mathbf1_{A_i}$, an <indicator random variable>, and $N=\sum_iI_i$. Since $\mathbb E[I_i]=\mathbb P(A_i)$, we get $\mathbb E[N]=\sum_i\mathbb P(A_i)$. There is no need to compute the whole <probability distribution> of $N$, or to assume <independence>. For a <uniform random permutation> of $n\geq1$ objects, each object stays fixed with <probability> $1/n$, so the <fixed point count of a uniform random permutation> has <expected value> one for every $n$. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/6-042j-mathematics-for-computer-science-spring-2015/mit6_042js15_textbook.pdf[Lehman, Leighton and Meyer, Mathematics for Computer Science, §18.5: linearity and sums of indicators]{ref}.

<Linearity of expectation> concerns <addition> and multiplication by fixed <scalars>. Products instead satisfy $\mathbb E[XY]=\mathbb E[X]\mathbb E[Y]$ under the hypotheses of <expectation of a product of independent random variables>. For the example above, $\mathbb E[X^2]=14.5$, whereas $(\mathbb E[X])^2=2.89$. Applying a nonlinear <function> before averaging can therefore change the answer substantially; use the <law of the unconscious statistician> to compute that average.

= Variance of a sample mean
{parent=Expected value}

For independent identically distributed variables of variance $\sigma^2$, the mean of $n$ samples has variance $\sigma^2/n$.

= Tail integral formula for moments
{parent=Expected value}

For $q>0$ and a <random variable> $X$ with finite absolute $q$th moment, <Tonelli theorem> gives
$$
\mathbb E|X|^q=q\int_0^\infty t^{q-1}\mathbb P(|X|\geq t)\,dt.
$$

= Tail integral formula for expectation
{parent=Tail integral formula for moments}
{title2=$\mathbb EY=\int_0^\infty\mathbb P(Y>t)dt$}

For a nonnegative <random variable>, $Y=\int_0^\infty\mathbf1_{\{Y>t\}}dt$ pointwise, including when $Y=\infty$. <Tonelli's theorem> permits taking <expectation> under the integral and proves the formula in the extended nonnegative reals. This relates finiteness of a <Brownian exit time> <expectation> to integrability of its survival probability.

= Second moment bound from a clipped Gaussian tail
{parent=Tail integral formula for moments}
{title2=$\mathbb E Z^2\leq(1+\log A)/b$}

If $\mathbb P(|Z|\geq t)\leq A e^{-bt^2}$ for $A\geq1$ and $b>0$, then $\mathbb E Z^2\leq(1+\log A)/b$. Indeed the <tail integral formula for moments> gives $\mathbb E Z^2\leq\int_0^\infty\min(1,Ae^{-bu})\,du$; split at $u=(\log A)/b$. Clipping at one improves the constant compared with integrating the exponential bound alone.

\Include[probability-inequality]{parent=Probability theory}

= Conditional probability
{title2=$P(A\mid B)$}
{parent=Probability theory}
{wiki}

For <events> $A,B$ with $\mathbb P(B)>0$, conditional probability is
$$
\mathbb P(A\mid B)=\frac{\mathbb P(A\cap B)}{\mathbb P(B)}.
$$
Restrict attention to the outcomes in $B$, then renormalize their <probability measure> so its total mass becomes one. The resulting rule $A\mapsto\mathbb P(A\mid B)$ is itself a <probability measure>. The definition and its connection to <independent events> are developed in https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-05-introduction-to-probability-and-statistics-spring-2022/resources/mit18_05_s22_class03-prep_pdf/[MIT 18.05, Reading 3: Conditional Probability, Independence and Bayes' Theorem].

For the two-dice model, let $A$ mean the sum is at least nine and $B$ mean the first result is six. Of the six outcomes in $B$, four belong to $A$: the second die can be 3, 4, 5, or 6. Thus **$\mathbb P(A\mid B)=2/3$**. Reversing the information gives a different answer: four of the ten outcomes in $A$ have first result six, so **$\mathbb P(B\mid A)=2/5$**. Both ratios use the same overlap; their denominators describe different information.

\Image[conditional-probability-dice.png]
{title=Conditioning selects a different denominator in the same dice outcome grid}

The definition requires positive conditioning mass. For a continuously distributed <random variable> $X$, it cannot directly define $\mathbb P(A\mid X=x)$ when $\mathbb P(X=x)=0$. A <regular conditional distribution> or <conditional density> supplies the appropriate extension, with the usual <almost everywhere> qualification. Nor does conditioning by itself justify <causal inference>: changing which outcomes are considered is different from physically changing how the experiment is generated.

= Three prisoners problem
{parent=Conditional probability}
{wiki}

A messenger names an excluded candidate while concealing the uniquely selected one. The <conditional probability> depends on the naming rule. With three equally likely selected candidates and a messenger forbidden to name the observing candidate A, let $s$ be the probability of naming B when A is selected. When C is selected the messenger must name B, and when B is selected cannot name B. Thus the probability that A is not selected, conditional on B being named, is $1/(1+s)$. Unbiased naming has $s=1/2$ and leaves this probability $2/3$; bias toward B gives $1/2$, and bias toward C gives one. Conditioning on the name is different from conditioning only on B's exclusion.

= Chain rule for probabilities
{parent=Conditional probability}
{title2=$P(A_1\cap\cdots\cap A_m)=\prod_jP(A_j\mid A_1\cap\cdots\cap A_{j-1})$}

When all conditioning events have positive <probability>, repeatedly apply the definition of <conditional probability> to factor a joint event into successive conditional factors. For a sequence of discrete responses, this yields the product of conditional masses along the actual outcome path. It requires no unconditional independence assumption.

= Disintegration theorem for a probability measure
{parent=Conditional probability}
{title2=$\mu=\int\mu_y\,d(\pi_*\mu)(y)$}

For a <probability measure> on a standard Borel space and a measurable map $\pi$ to another standard Borel space, there is an almost-everywhere unique <measurable probability kernel> $y\mapsto\mu_y$ supported on $\pi^{-1}\{y\}$. It integrates to $\mu$, and $\int f\,d\mu_{\pi(x)}$ realizes <conditional expectation> given $\pi$. These measures are the <conditional measures of a factor>.

= Conditional measures of a factor
{parent=Disintegration theorem for a probability measure}
{title2=$\mu_y$}

= Conditional measure
{synonym}

The fiber-supported <measurable probability kernel> supplied by the <disintegration theorem for a probability measure> for a <factor map>. For integrable $f$, $\mathbb E[f\mid\varphi^{-1}\mathcal B_Y](x)=\int f\,d\mu_{\varphi(x)}$ <almost everywhere>. The same construction applies to general measurable maps and countably generated sub-sigma-algebras modulo null sets.

= Measurable probability kernel
{parent=Conditional probability}
{title2=$y\mapsto K_y$}

A family of probability measures $K_y$ on a measurable space for which $y\mapsto K_y(A)$ is measurable for every measurable set $A$. This combines measurable dependence on a parameter with a <probability measure> at each parameter. The <conditional measures of a factor> form such a kernel, with the additional fiber-support property from disintegration.

= Positive association of two events
{parent=Conditional probability}
{title2=$P(A\cap B)>P(A)P(B)$}

For nontrivial <events>, strict attraction under <conditional probability> is equivalent to a positive covariance of their indicator variables. This condition is symmetric in the two <events>. Complementing exactly one of the <events> reverses the strict inequality. This two-event condition should be distinguished from association of an entire family of random variables.

= Bayes formula for conditional expectation
{parent=Conditional probability}

If $d\widetilde{\mathbb P}=Z\,d\mathbb P$ and $Z_t=\mathbb E[Z\mid\mathcal F_t]>0$, then
$$
\widetilde{\mathbb E}[X\mid\mathcal F_t]
=\frac{\mathbb E[ZX\mid\mathcal F_t]}{Z_t}.
$$

= Bayes' theorem
{c}
{parent=Conditional probability}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mathbb P(B\mid A)=\mathbb P(A\mid B)\mathbb P(B)/\mathbb P(A)$}

For a finite <measurable partition> $(B_j)$ with positive <probabilities>, and an <event> $A$ of positive <probability>,
$$
\mathbb P(B_j\mid A)
=\frac{\mathbb P(A\mid B_j)\mathbb P(B_j)}
{\sum_k\mathbb P(A\mid B_k)\mathbb P(B_k)}.
$$
Its beauty is that a model predicting observations from a hidden state can also describe uncertainty about the state after an observation. Each numerator is a joint <probability>; the denominator, supplied by the <law of total probability>, makes the answers sum to one. This is a direct consequence of <conditional probability>, rather than an extra axiom.

Select the coin from <law of total probability> once, then toss that same coin repeatedly, with <conditionally independent> tosses given its identity. Let $B$ denote the $3/4$-head coin, initially of <prior probability> $1/5$. After one head $H$, its <posterior probability> is
$$
\boxed{\mathbb P(B\mid H)=\frac{(1/5)(3/4)}{(1/5)(3/4)+(4/5)(1/2)}=\frac3{11}.}
$$
The <likelihood> of a head is larger under $B$, but $B$ remains less likely than the other coin because its <prior probability> was smaller. A large <likelihood> for an observation does not itself imply a large <posterior probability> for the hypothesis.

After an ordered sequence containing $h$ heads and $t$ tails, its <likelihood ratio> between the two coins is $(3/2)^h(1/2)^t$. Bayes' theorem therefore gives <posterior odds>
$$
O_{B:B^c}=\frac14\left(\frac32\right)^h\left(\frac12\right)^t,\qquad
\mathbb P(B\mid\text{sequence})=\frac{O_{B:B^c}}{1+O_{B:B^c}}.
$$
Each head multiplies the <odds> by $3/2$ and each tail by $1/2$. Three heads give <posterior probability> $27/59$, still below one half; four give $81/145$, above one half. The finite-model update and the distinction between <prior probability>, <likelihood> and <posterior probability> are set out in https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-05-introduction-to-probability-and-statistics-spring-2022/mit18_05_s22_statistics.pdf[MIT 18.05, Reading 11: Bayesian Updating with Discrete Priors].

\Image[bayes-coin-updating.png]
{title=Updating uncertainty about one selected coin after repeated observations}

The hidden selection also creates dependence. Each toss has marginal head <probability> $11/20$, but two heads have joint <probability> $(4/5)(1/2)^2+(1/5)(3/4)^2=5/16$, greater than $(11/20)^2=121/400$. Their indicator <covariance> is $1/100$. Seeing a head makes the head-favoring coin more likely, so it also raises the chance of a future head. This is <covariance induced by a shared latent variable>, despite <conditional independence> of the tosses given the coin.

Historically, Thomas Bayes's essay on an unknown success <probability> appeared in 1763 after his death, communicated by Richard Price. Its opening problem asks how observed successes and failures constrain that unknown <probability>; Price's introductory letter explains the connection to reasoning from experience. That is a more substantial inference problem than simply rearranging today's elementary identity. https://www.gtfp.cs.rhul.ac.uk/pulskamp/Bayes/bayesessay-rjp.pdf[Bayes, An Essay towards solving a Problem in the Doctrine of Chances, 1763; original text transcribed, introductory letter and statement of the problem]{ref}

= Posterior coin count after exactly one head
{parent=Bayes' theorem}
{title2=$P(N=n\mid H=1)=n(1-r)^2r^{n-1}$}

Let $N$ have the positive-integer <geometric distribution> with success parameter $p\in(0,1)$, and conditionally toss $N$ independent coins with head <probability> $\theta\in(0,1)$. Exactly one head has conditional <probability> $n\theta(1-\theta)^{n-1}$. Combining this with the prior by <Bayes theorem> gives the displayed posterior with $r=(1-p)(1-\theta)$; its normalization uses $\sum_{n\geq1}nr^{n-1}=(1-r)^{-2}$. For $p=\theta=1/2$, the posterior is $9n/4^{n+1}$. It is also a shifted <negative binomial distribution>, with $N-1$ equal in law to the number of failures before two successes of <probability> $1-r$.

= Posterior equality for proportional likelihoods
{parent=Bayes' theorem}
{title2=$L_i'=hL_i\ \Longrightarrow\ \pi_i'=\pi_i$}

If every model's <likelihood> is multiplied by the same positive factor independent of its parameter or model index, <Bayes theorem> cancels that factor from the normalized posterior. Fixed-count Bernoulli sampling and sampling stopped at a specified positive head count give such proportional <likelihoods> for the same total heads and tails. This conclusion uses their actual sampling <likelihoods>; it is not a claim that every selection or stopping rule can be ignored.

= Posterior coin selection from repeated tosses
{parent=Bayes' theorem}
{title2=$P(B\mid H^m)=\pi p_B^m/[(1-\pi)p_A^m+\pi p_B^m]$}

If a hidden coin is selected once with prior probability $\pi$ of coin $B$, repeated tosses are <independent> conditional on that coin. <Bayes theorem> weights the prior by each all-heads likelihood. They need not be <independent> without conditioning on the chosen coin.

= Conditional distribution
{parent=Conditional probability}
{wiki}

A conditional distribution is the probability law of one random quantity given information about another.

= Regular conditional distribution
{parent=Conditional distribution}
{title2=$K(y,B)=P(X\in B\mid Y=y)$}

A regular conditional distribution of a <random variable> $X$ given $Y$ is a <Markov kernel> $K$ such that, for all measurable sets $A,B$,
$$
P(X\in B,Y\in A)=\int_A K(y,B)\,P_Y(dy).
$$
For each $y$, $K(y,\cdot)$ is a <probability measure>; for each $B$, $K(\cdot,B)$ is measurable. Thus it assigns an actual probability distribution to a specified observation even when $P(Y=y)=0$. Such kernels exist for standard Borel state spaces, including real-valued variables, and their uniqueness is only up to $P_Y$-null sets. With a joint density and positive marginal density, the ratio of the two densities defines the usual conditional density kernel. Values outside the marginal support or on a null set are not uniquely determined by the joint law.

= Conditional density
{parent=Conditional distribution}
{title2=$f_{Y\mid X=x}(y)$}

= Conditional probability density
{synonym}

Where the <marginal density> $f_X(x)$ is positive, a <joint probability density> determines the conditional density
$$
f_{Y\mid X=x}(y)=\frac{f_{X,Y}(x,y)}{f_X(x)}.
$$
It integrates to one in $y$, and supplies <conditional expectations> by integrating against it. Its values on a probability-zero set of conditioning points are not fixed by the <joint probability distribution>.

= Full conditional distribution
{parent=Conditional distribution}

A full conditional distribution is the conditional law of one component of a joint model given all the other components and the observed data. A <Gibbs sampler> updates components by drawing from these laws.

= Random partition
{parent=Probability theory}
{wiki}

A random partition is a probability distribution on partitions of a finite or countable set.

= Poisson-Dirichlet distribution
{parent=Random partition}
{c}
{wiki}

The Poisson-Dirichlet family consists of laws of decreasing random mass partitions $(p_i)$ with $\sum_i p_i=1$. Its two-parameter notation is $PD(\alpha,\theta)$, with $0\le\alpha<1$ and $\theta>-\alpha$. The conventional one-parameter <probability distribution> $PD(\theta)$ means $PD(0,\theta)$, for $\theta>0$, and can be constructed from normalized gamma jumps.

= One-parameter Poisson-Dirichlet distribution
{parent=Poisson-Dirichlet distribution}
{title2=$PD(\theta)=PD(0,\theta)$}

Take a <Poisson random measure> on $(0,\infty)$ with intensity $\theta e^{-u}du/u$ and arrange its atoms as $J_1>J_2>\cdots$. The sum $T=\sum_iJ_i$ is finite and positive <almost surely>, has <Gamma distribution> with shape $\theta$ and rate one, and the law of $(J_i/T)$ is $PD(\theta)$. The order is decreasing, so the first component is the largest; a size-biased selection of a component has a different law.

= Largest component of a Poisson-Dirichlet partition
{parent=One-parameter Poisson-Dirichlet distribution}
{title2=$F_\theta(a)=\mathbb P(p_1\le a)$}

For $a\ge1/2$, at most one component exceeds $a$, so $1-F_\theta(a)=\theta\int_a^1x^{-1}(1-x)^{\theta-1}dx$. Below one half several components can exceed the threshold. Put $N_a=\#\{i:p_i>a\}$ and use the finite identity $\mathbf1_{\{N_a=0\}}=\sum_m(-1)^m(N_a)_m/m!$, with the <factorial moment densities of Poisson-Dirichlet components>. It gives the full <probability distribution> as finite <inclusion-exclusion> over $x_i>a$, $\sum_i x_i<1$. In contrast, a size-biased component is beta-distributed.

= Factorial moment densities of Poisson-Dirichlet components
{parent=One-parameter Poisson-Dirichlet distribution}
{title2=$\alpha_\theta^{(m)}(dx)=\theta^m\frac{(1-\sum_i x_i)^{\theta-1}}{\prod_i x_i}\,dx$}

This density is supported where all $x_i>0$ and $\sum_i x_i<1$, and gives the expected sum over ordered distinct components of $PD(\theta)$. Apply the iterated <Mecke formula for a Poisson random measure> to $m$ selected jumps $u_i$ and the remaining gamma total $t$. With $s=t+\sum_i u_i$ and $x_i=u_i/s$, the Jacobian is $s^m$ and the integral in $s$ is $\Gamma(\theta)$, giving the density. It describes joint counts of large components, beyond the first-moment measure.

= Mean measure of Poisson-Dirichlet components
{parent=One-parameter Poisson-Dirichlet distribution}
{title2=$\alpha_\theta(dx)=\theta x^{-1}(1-x)^{\theta-1}\,dx$}

For $PD(\theta)$, $\mathbb E\sum_i\varphi(p_i)=\int\varphi\,d\alpha_\theta$ whenever the absolute integral is finite. Apply the <Mecke formula for a Poisson random measure> to a selected jump $u$ and the remaining total $t$, whose density is $t^{\theta-1}e^{-t}/\Gamma(\theta)$. Changing variables to $s=u+t$ and $x=u/s$ factors out a <Gamma function> and gives the displayed measure. Weighting it by $x$ gives a <probability> density $\theta(1-x)^{\theta-1}$, the <Beta distribution> of a size-biased component.

= Restricted-growth string
{parent=Random partition}

A restricted-growth string encodes a set partition by numbering blocks in order of first appearance.

= Singleton block
{parent=Random partition}

A singleton block is a block containing exactly one element.

= Exchangeable random partition
{parent=Random partition}
{wiki}

An exchangeable random partition has a law invariant under finite permutations of the underlying labels.

= Random path in a binary tree
{parent=Probability theory}

In a fair random root-to-leaf path, a specified leaf at depth $d$ is reached with probability $2^{-d}$.

\Include[markov-process]{parent=Probability theory}

= Renewal process
{parent=Probability theory}
{wiki}

A renewal process with independent identically distributed nonnegative interarrival times $\xi_1,\xi_2,\ldots$ has renewal epochs
$$
S_0=0,\qquad S_n=\xi_1+\cdots+\xi_n,
$$
and counting process $N_t=\max\{n:S_n\leq t\}$.

= Alternating split of a Poisson process
{parent=Renewal process}
{title2=$X_B(t)=\lceil N(t)/2\rceil,\quad X_C(t)=\lfloor N(t)/2\rfloor$}

Alternately routing a rate-$r$ <Poisson process> produces non-Poisson streams with common interarrival law Erlang$(2,r)$. The odd-numbered stream has an initial exponential delay, while the even-numbered stream is an ordinary renewal process. Their equilibrium delay survivor is $e^{-rt}(1+rt/2)$. The probability of no odd-stream point in $(s,s+t]$ is $e^{-rt}[1+rt(1-e^{-2rs})/2]$; for the even stream replace the minus by a plus. Conditioning on the parity of $N(s)$ proves these formulas.

= Uniform-sum crossing time below one
{parent=Renewal process}
{title2=$\mathbb E N(x)=e^x\quad(0\leq x\leq1)$}

For independent <uniform distribution> increments on $(0,1)$ and $0\leq x\leq1$, let $N(x)$ be the first index at which their partial sum exceeds $x$. <First-step analysis> gives $M(x)=\mathbb E N(x)=1+\int_0^xM(s)ds$, hence $M=e^x$. Equivalently, $\mathbb P(N(x)>n)=x^n/n!$ is a simplex-volume probability and the <tail-sum formula> sums to $e^x$. The strict inequality gives $N(0)=1$ almost surely.

= Renewal measure
{parent=Renewal process}
{title2=$U=\sum_{j\geq0}K^{*j}$}

For a positive interarrival law $K$, the renewal measure $U=\sum_{j\geq0}K^{*j}$ counts the expected number of renewal epochs in a set, including the epoch at zero through $K^{*0}=\delta_0$. Its cumulative function is one plus the usual <renewal function>. Convolution against $U$ solves a <renewal equation>.

= Key renewal theorem
{parent=Renewal process}
{title2=$g*U(t)\longrightarrow(\mathbb ET)^{-1}\int_0^\infty g$}

For positive nonarithmetic interarrival times with law $K$ and mean $m\in(0,\infty]$, let $U=\sum_{j\geq0}K^{*j}$ be their <renewal measure>. If $g$ is <directly Riemann integrable>, then $\int_{[0,t]}g(t-x)U(dx)\to m^{-1}\int_0^\infty g(x)\,dx$, with $1/\infty=0$. Thus a locally bounded solution of $z=g+z*K$ has this limit through its <renewal representation>. Arithmetic laws require the corresponding lattice version.

= Renewal equation
{parent=Renewal process}
{title2=$u_n=\sum_{k=1}^n f_k u_{n-k},\ u_0=1$}

A renewal equation decomposes a process at its first renewal. For returns of a <Markov chain> to a fixed state, $f_k$ is the first-return probability and $u_n$ the probability of being at that state at time $n$. Their <probability generating functions> satisfy $U=1+FU$.

= Renewal representation
{parent=Renewal equation}
{title2=$z=g*U$}

For positive interarrivals with law $K$, the unique locally bounded solution of $z=g+z*K$ is $z=g*U=\sum_{j\geq0}g*K^{*j}$, where $U$ is the <renewal measure>. Iteration proves the identity: the residual is bounded on each compact interval by a constant times the probability that the sum of many positive interarrivals stays in that interval, which tends to zero.

= Defective renewal equation
{parent=Renewal equation}
{title2=$z=g+z*Q,\quad Q([0,\infty))<1$}

A defective renewal equation is $z(u)=g(u)+\int_{[0,u]}z(u-x)Q(dx)$ where the nonnegative kernel measure has total mass less than one. Its <renewal representation> is a convergent sum of convolutions. <Exponential tilting> can turn the kernel into a probability measure, enabling the <key renewal theorem>.

= Exponential tilt of the ruin renewal kernel
{parent=Defective renewal equation}
{title2=$k_R(x)=(\lambda/c)e^{Rx}\overline F(x)$}

The <ultimate ruin probability> satisfies a tail-forced <defective renewal equation>. Multiplying it by $e^{Ru}$ at an <adjustment coefficient> turns its kernel into the probability density $k_R$. Normalization is exactly $\lambda(M(R)-1)=cR$. The density is absolutely continuous even when the original claim law has atoms; thus it has a <nonarithmetic distribution>.

= Exponential moment of a renewal count
{parent=Renewal process}
{title2=$\mathbb E e^{\theta N(t)}$}

For nonnegative finite <independent and identically distributed random variables> as inter-arrivals with positive probability of exceeding $\varepsilon>0$, write $p=\mathbb P(X_1\geq\varepsilon)>0$. The count tail is bounded by $\mathbb P(\operatorname{Bin}(n,p)\leq\lfloor t/\varepsilon\rfloor)$, which has geometric decay times a polynomial in $n$. Hence $\mathbb E e^{\theta N(t)}<\infty$ for every fixed finite $t$ and sufficiently small $\theta>0$.

= Renewal function
{title2=$m(t)$}
{parent=Renewal process}

The renewal function is the expected number of renewals by time $t$:
$$
m(t)=\mathbb E N_t.
$$

= Elementary renewal theorem
{parent=Renewal function}

For strictly positive independent identically distributed interarrival times of finite mean $\mu$,
$$
\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{\mathbb EN_t}{t}=\frac1\mu.
$$

= Renewal-reward process
{parent=Renewal process}

A renewal-reward process attaches an integrable reward $R_n$ to each i.i.d. renewal cycle of length $\xi_n$. Its cumulative reward sums completed-cycle rewards and may include reward accrued in the current unfinished cycle.

= Renewal-reward theorem
{parent=Renewal-reward process}
{wiki}

For independent identically distributed regenerative cycles with $0<\mathbb E\xi_1<\infty$ and $\mathbb E|R_1|<\infty$, the long-run reward rate is
$$
\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{Z(t)}t
=\frac{\mathbb ER_1}{\mathbb E\xi_1}
$$
almost surely, provided the unfinished-cycle reward is negligible after division by $t$.

= Long-run availability under periodic inspection
{parent=Renewal-reward theorem}

If a repaired machine has independent exponential lifetimes of rate $\lambda$ and is inspected and repaired every $m$ time units after each repair, then its cycle length is $m\lceil X/m\rceil$ and its working reward is $X$. Its long-run working proportion is
$$
\frac{1-e^{-\lambda m}}{\lambda m}.
$$

= Renewal counting law of large numbers
{parent=Renewal process}

If $0<\mathbb E\xi_1=1/\lambda<\infty$, then the strong law $S_n/n\to1/\lambda$ and the inequalities $S_{N_t}\leq t<S_{N_t+1}$ imply $N_t/t\to\lambda$ almost surely.

= Renewal interval containing a fixed time
{parent=Renewal process}

The renewal interval containing $t$ has length
$$
L(t)=S_{N_t+1}-S_{N_t}=\xi_{N_t+1}.
$$
Selection by a fixed observation time favours longer interarrival times.

= Size-biased distribution
{parent=Renewal interval containing a fixed time}
{wiki}

For a nonnegative random variable $X$ with $0<\mathbb EX<\infty$, its size-biased version $\widehat X$ is defined by
$$
\mathbb E h(\widehat X)=\frac{\mathbb E[Xh(X)]}{\mathbb EX}
$$
for bounded measurable $h$. If $X$ has density $f$, then $\widehat X$ has density $xf(x)/\mathbb EX$.

= Exponential renewal interval limit
{parent=Renewal interval containing a fixed time}

For exponential interarrival times of rate $\lambda$, the age and residual life at time $t$ converge jointly to independent rate-$\lambda$ exponential variables. Their sum therefore converges to the gamma distribution with density $\lambda^2xe^{-\lambda x}$, the size-biased exponential law.

= Stochastic length bias of a renewal interval
{parent=Renewal interval containing a fixed time}

At every deterministic $t>0$, the containing interval stochastically dominates an unselected interarrival time:
$$
\mathbb P(L(t)\geq x)\geq\mathbb P(\xi_1\geq x),\qquad x>0.
$$
Conditioning on the first interarrival gives a renewal equation; subtracting the constant tail probability leaves a renewal equation with a nonnegative forcing term.

= Residual lifetime process
{parent=Renewal process}
{wiki}

The residual lifetime is the time remaining until the next renewal.

= Renewal excess limit theorem
{parent=Residual lifetime process}

For non-arithmetic inter-renewal times with finite positive mean $\mu$, the excess $E(t)=S_{N(t)+1}-t$ converges in distribution to $E_\infty$ with
$$
\mathbb P(E_\infty>x)
=\frac1\mu\int_x^\infty\mathbb P(\xi_1>y)\,dy.
$$

= Exponential first interarrival in a renewal superposition containing a Poisson process
{parent=Renewal excess limit theorem}

If an independent superposition of a Poisson process and a non-arithmetic finite-mean renewal process is itself a renewal process, then its first interarrival is exponential. Equating the limiting excess law computed as a renewal excess with the minimum of the Poisson and second-process excesses yields an integral equation whose survival-function solutions are exponential.

= Residual lifetime Markov chain
{parent=Residual lifetime process}
{wiki}

With bounded integer lifetimes, residual lifetime decreases deterministically and resets randomly at zero.

= Equilibrium residual-life distribution
{parent=Residual lifetime process}
{wiki}

The invariant residual-life mass at i is proportional to the probability that a lifetime exceeds i.

= Equilibrium delay of an Erlang renewal process
{parent=Equilibrium residual-life distribution}

For inter-renewal times with an <Erlang distribution> of integer shape $r$ and rate $\lambda$, the mean is $r/\lambda$ and survival function is $e^{-\lambda t}\sum_{j=0}^{r-1}(\lambda t)^j/j!$. The equilibrium first-delay density is survival divided by the mean,
$$
f_e(t)=\frac\lambda r e^{-\lambda t}\sum_{j=0}^{r-1}\frac{(\lambda t)^j}{j!},\qquad t>0.
$$
This is the equal mixture of Erlang distributions of shapes $1,\ldots,r$. It can be constructed by observing a Poisson clock with uniformly randomized phase modulo $r$, whose every $r$th event is a renewal.

= Law of total variance
{parent=Probability theory}
{title2=$\operatorname{Var}(X)=\mathbb E[\operatorname{Var}(X\mid Y)]+\operatorname{Var}(\mathbb E[X\mid Y])$}
{wiki}

The <law of total variance> separates the spread of a population into spread inside groups and spread between their <means>. For a real <square-integrable> <random variable> $X$, so that $\mathbb E[X^2]<\infty$, and another <random variable> $Y$ on the same <probability space>,
$$
\boxed{\operatorname{Var}(X)=\mathbb E[\operatorname{Var}(X\mid Y)]+\operatorname{Var}(\mathbb E[X\mid Y]).}
$$
The first term averages the <conditional variance> over the possible information $Y$; the second measures how much the <conditional mean> changes with that information. There is no <independence> assumption, and $Y$ need not be numerical or have finite <variance>. Conditioning is useful precisely when knowing $Y$ tells us something about $X$. See https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/6-041sc-probabilistic-systems-analysis-and-applied-probability-fall-2013/2aae0a59cd484849fec4aa12a6f18969_MIT6_041SCF13_Law_of_Total_Variance_300k.pdf[MIT tutorial on using conditional expectation and variance].

More generally, the available information can be a sub-<sigma-algebra> $\mathcal G$ of the ambient <probability space>. With $m=\mathbb E[X\mid\mathcal G]$, the existing <conditional expectation> definition gives
$$
\operatorname{Var}(X\mid\mathcal G)=\mathbb E[(X-m)^2\mid\mathcal G],\qquad
\operatorname{Var}(X)=\mathbb E[\operatorname{Var}(X\mid\mathcal G)]+\operatorname{Var}(m).
$$
Taking $\mathcal G=\sigma(Y)$ recovers the displayed formula. The <conditional mean> and <conditional variance> are themselves <random variables>: before learning the group, we do not yet know which group average or group spread will apply. Their outer <expectation> and <variance> turn these varying quantities into numbers.

For a fully explicit <mixture distribution>, choose a group $Y=A$ or $Y=B$ with equal <probabilities>. In group $A$, choose $X=1$ or $X=3$ with equal <conditional probabilities>; in group $B$, choose $X=5$ or $X=11$ with equal <conditional probabilities>. Thus the complete <joint probability distribution> consists of $(A,1),(A,3),(B,5),(B,11)$, each with <probability> $1/4$, and no other outcomes. The <conditional means> are $2$ and $8$. Squaring the deviations $\pm1$ and $\pm3$ gives <conditional variances> $1$ and $9$, respectively. The <law of total expectation> gives the overall <mean> $\mu=(2+8)/2=5$.

The within-group contribution to <variance> is
$$
\mathbb E[\operatorname{Var}(X\mid Y)]=\tfrac12(1)+\tfrac12(9)=5.
$$
For the between-group contribution, the <conditional mean> takes values $2$ and $8$, each three units from the overall <mean>. Consequently,
$$
\operatorname{Var}(\mathbb E[X\mid Y])=\tfrac12(2-5)^2+\tfrac12(8-5)^2=9,
\qquad \operatorname{Var}(X)=5+9=14.
$$
We can check this without any conditioning: the <discrete random variable> $X$ takes values $1,3,5,11$ with equal <probabilities>, so
$$
\operatorname{Var}(X)=\frac{(1-5)^2+(3-5)^2+(5-5)^2+(11-5)^2}{4}
=\frac{16+4+0+36}{4}=14.
$$
Equivalently, its <second moment> is $(1+9+25+121)/4=39$, and subtracting the squared <mean> gives $39-25=14$. These are population <variances>, with the stated <probabilities> as weights, rather than estimates using the corrected denominator of a <sample variance>.

\Image[total-variance-mixture.png]
{title=Four equally likely outcomes split total variance into within-group 5 and between-group 9}

The figure makes the two sources of spread visible. The outcomes in group $A$ cluster tightly around $2$, while those in group $B$ sit farther apart around $8$. Replacing every outcome by its <conditional mean> removes the internal spread but leaves the separation between groups. Conversely, subtracting each group's <conditional mean> aligns the two groups around zero. Averaging only the <conditional variances> would miss nine units of <variance>; retaining only the <conditional means> would miss five. Neither summary alone describes the whole population.

Two modifications clarify what each contribution measures. Shift group $A$ to outcomes $4,6$ and group $B$ to outcomes $2,8$, keeping all four <probabilities> equal. Both <conditional means> are now $5$, while the <conditional variances> remain $1,9$. The between-group contribution vanishes, and total <variance> is just $5$. Group membership still tells us how uncertain an outcome is, even though the best point prediction stays the same. Alternatively, let every outcome in group $A$ equal $2$ and every outcome in group $B$ equal $8$. Now both <conditional variances> vanish, and all nine units of total <variance> come from group separation. Knowing the group predicts the outcome perfectly. These limiting cases distinguish differences in average outcome from differences in internal spread; the original <mixture distribution> contains both.

The same calculation answers a prediction question. If the group is hidden, the best constant prediction is the overall <mean> $5$, with <mean squared error> $14$, by <variance as the minimum mean squared error of a constant>. If the group is revealed, predict $2$ for $A$ and $8$ for $B$. The errors become $-1,1,-3,3$, and their <mean squared error> is $5$. Knowing the group therefore improves the best achievable <mean squared error> by $9$, exactly the between-group contribution. This is an interpretation relative to the information available: the remaining uncertainty could shrink further if additional information were revealed.

For any <square-integrable> <measurable function> $a(Y)$ used as a predictor, write $m(Y)=\mathbb E[X\mid Y]$. The prediction identity is
$$
\mathbb E[(X-a(Y))^2]=\mathbb E[\operatorname{Var}(X\mid Y)]+\mathbb E[(m(Y)-a(Y))^2].
$$
Thus predicting with the <regression function> $m(y)=\mathbb E[X\mid Y=y]$ gives the <conditional expectation> $m(Y)$ and minimizes <mean squared error> over every permitted predictor, including nonlinear ones. This minimizes average squared error, not the error for each individual outcome. See https://www.stat.berkeley.edu/~aditya/resources/AllLectures2018Fall201A.pdf#page=71[Berkeley Statistics 201A notes, sections 36–37].

Why do the two pieces add cleanly? Put $R=X-m(Y)$. Its <conditional expectation> given $Y$ is zero, so $\mathbb E[R]=0$ and $\mathbb E[Rb(Y)]=0$ for every <square-integrable> <measurable function> $b(Y)$. In particular, $R$ and $m(Y)$ are <uncorrelated random variables>. Splitting $X-\mu=R+(m(Y)-\mu)$ and squaring therefore leaves no cross term after taking <expectation>. Geometrically, the <conditional expectation> is an <orthogonal projection> in the <L2 space>, and the <law of total variance> is its <Pythagorean theorem> for centered <random variables>.

\Image[conditional-mean-residual.png]
{title=Conditional prediction leaves zero group means but different residual spreads}

In our example, the four products $R(m(Y)-5)$ are $3,-3,-9,9$, which average to zero. Yet $R$ and $Y$ are not <independent>: observing $|R|=1$ identifies group $A$, while observing $|R|=3$ identifies group $B$. This is a concrete instance of <heteroscedasticity>, with different <conditional variances> despite zero <conditional means> of the errors. A group can predict uncertainty even when it cannot predict the error's sign.

The <law of total variance> is useful whenever aggregated data conceal distinct groups. Changing <mixture weights> changes both contributions, so the weighting must match the population being described. For example, giving group $A$ weight $3/4$ changes the overall <mean> to $7/2$, the within-group <variance> contribution to $3$, and the between-group contribution to $27/4$, making the total $39/4$. Group differences account for spread and prediction gains; assigning them a causal explanation requires a separate <causal inference> argument.

= Laplace transform of a nonnegative random variable
{title2=$\psi_X(s)$}
{parent=Probability theory}

= Laplace transforms of nonnegative random variables
{synonym}
{c}

For a nonnegative <random variable> $X$, its Laplace transform is
$$
\psi_X(s)=\mathbb E[e^{-sX}],
\qquad s\geq0.
$$

= Second-moment expansion of a Laplace transform
{parent=Laplace transform of a nonnegative random variable}
{title2=$\mathbb Ee^{-\lambda X}=1-\lambda\mathbb EX+\lambda^2\mathbb EX^2/2+o(\lambda^2)$}

For nonnegative $X$ with finite <second moment>, <differentiation> under the <expectation> gives $\phi'(\lambda)=-\mathbb E[Xe^{-\lambda X}]$ for $\lambda>0$. The exponential Taylor remainder divided by $\lambda^2$ tends pointwise to zero and is bounded in absolute value by $X^2/2$. The <dominated convergence theorem> therefore proves the displayed expansion without requiring a third moment.

= Uniqueness theorem for Laplace transforms of nonnegative random variables
{parent=Laplace transform of a nonnegative random variable}

The <Laplace transform of a nonnegative random variable> determines its <probability law>. Indeed, $U=e^{-X}$ takes values in $(0,1]$, and $\mathbb E[U^n]=\psi_X(n)$. Thus the transform determines every moment of $U$; <moment determinacy on a compact interval> determines the law of $U$, and the injective inverse $X=-\log U$ determines the law of $X$.

= Characteristic function
{title2=$\varphi_X(t)$}
{parent=Probability theory}
{wiki=Characteristic_function_(probability_theory)}

The characteristic function of a real <random variable> $X$ is
$$
\varphi_X(t)=\mathbb E[e^{itX}].
$$
It determines the <probability distribution> of $X$.

= Characteristic-function derivative under an absolute first moment
{parent=Characteristic function}
{title2=$\phi\prime(0)=i\mathbb E X$}

If $\mathbb E|X|<\infty$, the <characteristic function> $\phi(u)=\mathbb E e^{iuX}$ satisfies $\phi'(0)=i\mathbb E X$. The bound $|(e^{iuX}-1)/u|\leq|X|$ and the <dominated convergence theorem> justify differentiation at zero. For mean zero, $\psi(u)=|1-\phi(u)|/|u|$ extends continuously by $\psi(0)=0$, and $|1-\phi(u)^n|\leq n|u|\psi(u)$ by a finite <geometric series>. This supplies a direct characteristic-function proof of the <weak law of large numbers>.

// Target: martingale.bigb

= Characteristic-function tail bound
{parent=Characteristic function}
{title2=$\mu(|y|\ge\lambda)\le\frac{\lambda}{1-\sin1}\int_0^{1/\lambda}(1-\operatorname{Re}\phi(u))du$}

Average $1-\cos(uy)$ over $0\le u\le1/\lambda$. Multiplication by $\lambda$ gives $1-\sin(y/\lambda)/(y/\lambda)$, with value zero at $y=0$. This is at least $1-\sin1$ when $|y|\ge\lambda$. <Tonelli's theorem> then gives the displayed bound for a <probability measure> with <characteristic function> $\phi$. The bound converts control of the <characteristic function> near zero into <tightness of probability measures> and is useful in proving the <Lévy continuity theorem>.

= Conditional characteristic function
{parent=Characteristic function}
{title2=$\mathbb E[e^{i\theta Y}\mid\mathcal G]$}

For a real <random variable> $Y$ and a sigma-algebra $\mathcal G$, the conditional characteristic function is $\mathbb E[e^{i\theta Y}\mid\mathcal G]$. If it equals the nonrandom <characteristic function> of a probability law for every $\theta$, then $Y$ has that law and is independent of $\mathcal G$. To verify the independence assertion, multiply the identity by any bounded $\mathcal G$-measurable test variable and use the <uniqueness theorem for characteristic functions> on the resulting finite measures. Equality for rational $\theta$ extends by continuity, so simultaneous null sets can be arranged when needed.

= Empirical characteristic function
{parent=Characteristic function}
{wiki}
{title2=$\varphi_n(u)$}

For a sample $X_1,\ldots,X_n$, $\varphi_n(u)=n^{-1}\sum_j e^{iuX_j}$ is the <characteristic function> of the sample's <empirical measure>. For <independent and identically distributed random variables>, $\mathbb E\varphi_n(u)=\varphi(u)$ and $\operatorname{Var}_{\mathbb C}\varphi_n(u)=(1-|\varphi(u)|^2)/n$, without any moment condition on the observations.

= Empirical characteristic process
{parent=Empirical characteristic function}
{title2=$\mathcal C_n(u)$}

The centered process $\mathcal C_n(u)=\sqrt n(\varphi_n(u)-\varphi(u))$ describes fluctuations of the <empirical characteristic function>. At a fixed frequency, its real and imaginary parts satisfy a <multivariate central limit theorem>. A process-level limit requires further control over the frequency index.

= Continuity of a characteristic function
{parent=Characteristic function}

Every characteristic function is uniformly continuous and satisfies $\varphi(0)=1$ and $|\varphi(t)|\leq1$. Continuity follows from the <dominated convergence theorem> applied to $e^{itX}$.

= Unit modulus of a characteristic function
{parent=Characteristic function}

If $|\varphi_X(u)|=1$, then $e^{iuX}$ is almost surely constant. Indeed,
$$
\mathbb E\left[\left|e^{iuX}-\mathbb E[e^{iuX}]\right|^2\right]
=1-|\varphi_X(u)|^2=0.
$$
Hence $uX\in\theta+2\pi\mathbb Z$ almost surely for some real $\theta$.

= Irrational unit-modulus frequencies force a point mass
{parent=Unit modulus of a characteristic function}

If $u/v$ is irrational and $|\varphi_X(u)|=|\varphi_X(v)|=1$, then $X$ is almost surely constant. For independent copies $X,X'$, both $u(X-X')$ and $v(X-X')$ lie in $2\pi\mathbb Z$ almost surely; irrationality makes the two corresponding lattices intersect only at zero, so $X=X'$ almost surely. Two independent identically distributed variables can agree almost surely only when their common law is a point mass.

= Characteristic function of a random vector
{title2=$\varphi_X(u)=\mathbb E[e^{i\langle u,X\rangle}]$}
{parent=Characteristic function}

For an $\mathbb R^d$-valued random vector, the characteristic function is $\varphi_X(u)=\mathbb E[e^{i\langle u,X\rangle}]$. It determines the joint distribution and satisfies $\varphi_{AX}(u)=\varphi_X(A^Tu)$ for every deterministic matrix $A$.

= Characteristic function of an isotropic Gaussian vector
{title2=$\exp(-\sigma^2\lVert u\rVert^2/2)$}
{parent=Characteristic function of a random vector}

If $X\sim N(0,\sigma^2I_d)$, then
$$
\varphi_X(u)=\exp\left(-\frac{\sigma^2}{2}\lVert u\rVert^2\right).
$$

= Orthogonally invariant random vector
{parent=Characteristic function of a random vector}

A random vector $X\in\mathbb R^d$ is orthogonally invariant when $AX$ has the same distribution as $X$ for every $A\in O(d)$. Its characteristic function is radial and real: $\varphi_X(u)=f(\lVert u\rVert^2)$ for a continuous function $f:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb R$.

= Maxwell characterization of the normal distribution
{c}
{parent=Orthogonally invariant random vector}

If an orthogonally invariant random vector in dimension at least two has independent coordinates, then it is centered isotropic Gaussian, possibly degenerate at zero. Independence and rotational invariance give
$$
f(r+s)=f(r)f(s),
$$
while continuity and $f(0)=1$ imply $f(r)=e^{-\alpha r}$ for some $\alpha\geq0$. Thus $X\sim N(0,2\alpha I_d)$.

= Uniqueness theorem for characteristic functions
{parent=Characteristic function}

Two finite Borel measures on $\mathbb R$ with the same characteristic function are equal. One proof convolves both measures with centered Gaussians, applies Fourier inversion to the smooth densities, and then lets the Gaussian variance tend to zero.

= Fourier-cutoff recovery of expectations from a characteristic function
{parent=Uniqueness theorem for characteristic functions}

Let $f$ be compactly supported and continuous, let $g,\widehat g\in L^1(\mathbb R)$, and use $\widehat g(t)=\int g(x)e^{itx}\,dx$. If $g(0)\ne0$, then
$$
\mathbb E[f(Z)]
=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}
\frac1{2\pi g(0)}
\int_{\mathbb R}g(\varepsilon s)\widehat f(-s)\varphi_Z(s)\,ds.
$$
This follows by <Fubini's theorem>, a change of variables, <Fourier inversion theorem>, and <dominated convergence theorem>. It gives a direct proof of the <uniqueness theorem for characteristic functions> because the right side depends only on $\varphi_Z$.

= Characteristic function of a sum of independent variables
{parent=Characteristic function}

Independence makes the characteristic function of a sum equal the product of the individual characteristic functions.

= Lévy continuity theorem
{c}
{parent=Characteristic function}
{wiki}

If the <characteristic functions> $\varphi_n$ of <probability distributions> $\mu_n$ converge pointwise to a function $\varphi$ that is continuous at zero, then $\varphi$ is a characteristic function and $\mu_n$ <convergence in distribution>[converges in distribution] to its distribution. Conversely, convergence in distribution implies pointwise convergence of the characteristic functions.

= Characteristic-function tightness bound
{parent=Lévy continuity theorem}
{title2=$\mu(|x|\geq2/h)\leq h^{-1}\int_{-h}^h(1-\operatorname{Re}\varphi(t))\,dt$}

For a <probability measure> on the real line with <characteristic function> $\varphi$, averaging $1-\cos(tx)$ over $[-h,h]$ gives $1-\sin(hx)/(hx)$. This is at least $1/2$ when $|x|\geq2/h$. Integrating gives the displayed bound. Consequently pointwise convergence of <characteristic functions> to a function continuous at zero implies <tightness of probability measures>, by the <dominated convergence theorem> on $[-h,h]$ and a separate tail bound for the finitely many initial measures.

= Weak law from a characteristic-function expansion
{parent=Lévy continuity theorem}

If <independent and identically distributed random variables> have <characteristic function> $\varphi(t)=1+iat+o(|t|)$ at zero, then their <sample mean> has <convergence in distribution> to $a$: its <characteristic function> is $\varphi(t/n)^n\to e^{iat}$. This is also <convergence in probability> by <convergence in distribution to a constant implies convergence in probability>.

= Variance normalization
{parent=Probability theory}

Dividing a centered sum by the square root of its total variance gives unit variance and is the natural central-limit scaling.

= Cox--Ross--Rubinstein model
{c}
{parent=Probability theory}

In the Cox--Ross--Rubinstein model, a stock changes at each discrete time by an up factor $U$ or a down factor $D$, while the money-market account changes by a deterministic factor $R$. After discounting by the money-market account, the factors are $u=U/R$ and $d=D/R$. The model is arbitrage-free exactly when $D<R<U$, equivalently $d<1<u$.

= Cox-Ross-Rubinstein diffusion approximation
{c}
{parent=Cox--Ross--Rubinstein model}
{title2=$u=e^{\sigma\sqrt h},\quad d=e^{-\sigma\sqrt h},\quad B_{k+1}/B_k=e^{\rho h}$}

= Binomial diffusion approximation
{synonym}

For mesh $h$, the risk-neutral up probability is $(e^{\rho h}-d)/(u-d)$. The log-price increment has mean $(\rho-\sigma^2/2)h+O(h^2)$ and variance $\sigma^2h+O(h^2)$. The <Donsker invariance principle> gives convergence of the log-price process to Brownian motion with this drift, hence of prices to the <Black-Scholes model>. Payoff-price convergence additionally uses continuity at almost every limiting path and uniform integrability for unbounded payoffs.

= Risk-neutral probability in the Cox--Ross--Rubinstein model
{c}
{parent=Cox--Ross--Rubinstein model}

The unique equivalent martingale measure in the one-stock Cox--Ross--Rubinstein model assigns each up move probability
$$
q=\frac{R-D}{U-D}=\frac{1-d}{u-d}
$$
and each down move probability $1-q$. Under this measure the discounted stock is a <martingale>.

= Risk-neutral valuation in the Cox--Ross--Rubinstein model
{c}
{parent=Risk-neutral probability in the Cox--Ross--Rubinstein model}

For a discounted claim $H$, the arbitrage-free value process is $V_t=\mathbb E_Q[H\mid\mathcal F_t]$. A terminal payoff $h(S_T)$ has value
$$
V_t=\sum_{k=0}^{T-t}\binom{T-t}{k}q^k(1-q)^{T-t-k}
h(S_tu^kd^{T-t-k}).
$$

= Running-maximum state reduction in the Cox--Ross--Rubinstein model
{c}
{parent=Risk-neutral valuation in the Cox--Ross--Rubinstein model}

For a payoff $h(S_T,M_T)$ with $M_t=\max_{s\leq t}S_s$, the pair $(S_t,M_t)$ is a sufficient Markov state for valuation. If an independent restarted stock path has initial value $y$, terminal value $z$, and running maximum $w$, then the continued payoff is
$$
g(x,m,y,z,w)=h\left(\frac{xz}{y},\max\left\{m,\frac{xw}{y}\right\}\right).
$$

= Thinning of a point process
{parent=Probability theory}

Thinning removes points from a <point process> according to a retention rule. Independent retention with probability $p(x)$ changes the intensity measure to $p(x)\mu(dx)$. For a <Poisson point process>, the retained and removed point processes are independent Poisson processes.
